4.3
Conjectures and remarks

In view of the results above, we conjecture that in all situations, in dimension 1, for the optimal diffusion coefficient:

•

The first three nonzero eigenvalues of the operator
-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}}
satisfy
\text{\char 21\relax}_{2}=\text{\char 21\relax}_{3}<\text{\char 21\relax}_{4}
.

•

The limiting procedure outlined in Section
3.2.2
to define the optimal diffusion by (
32
) holds; in particular
\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\alpha(\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\alpha}-\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha})=\eta\in[0,\eta^{\star}]
is well defined.

•

The independent and normalized eigenvectors
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
(associated to
\text{\char 21\relax}_{2}=\text{\char 21\relax}_{3}
) have derivatives that do not vanish at the same points and there are then two situations (see (
32
)):

–

either
\eta=\eta^{\star}
, which is equivalent to the fact that the optimal
diffusion vanishes at some points;

–

or
\eta<\eta^{\star}
, which is equivalent to the fact that the optimal
diffusion is uniformly positive.

It would be interesting to prove these conjectures, and also to understand which features of the potential
V
determine whether
\eta\in[0,\eta^{\star})
(the optimal diffusion is positive) or
\eta=\eta^{\star}
(the optimal diffusion vanishes at some point).
For example, we will see in the next section that when
V
is periodic with a period
1/k
where
k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
, the optimal diffusion converges rapidly to the optimal diffusion for the homogenized limit, which does not vanish.

5
Optimal diffusion matrix in the homogenized limit

A drawback of the optimization method described in
Section

4
is the computational burden associated with the calculation of the optimal diffusion matrix. To obtain an approximation of the optimal diffusion, which can be used for instance as a good initial guess to the optimization procedure, we propose a simple model for the effective diffusion of the optimized overdamped Langevin dynamics. This model is obtained by studying the asymptotic behavior of the optimal diffusion matrix in the homogenized limit of periodic potentials with infinitely small spatial periods. The limit is explicit either for linear constraints, or for generic
L^{p}_{V}
constraints in the one dimensional case
d=1
, and displays a simple dependence on the target density
\mu
. The form of this dependence is backed up by prior results in the literature
[
69
]
.

From a physical viewpoint, considering infinitely periodic materials is relevant, for example to study diffusions of adatoms on (periodic) surfaces or propagation of defects in (periodic) crystalline lattices
[
11
, Chapitre 3]
. We also refer to
Remark

7
where we explain that under a proper rescaling in time and space, the diffusion process can be seen as a solution to an effective Brownian motion on the torus whose diffusion is the homogenized diffusion. Maximizing this homogenized diffusion therefore amounts to enhancing the exploration capabilities of this limiting process.

In order to present our results, we start by introducing in
Section

5.1
the setting of homogenization theory, in particular the notion of
H
-convergence, and give some useful and standard results, adapted to our framework of periodic boundary conditions. Next, in
Section

5.2
, we study the homogenized limit of the diffusion matrices and then explicitly optimize the spectral gap of the homogenized limit for one-dimensional systems or linear constraints. In
Section

5.3
, we study the commutation of the homogenization and optimization procedures. We show that the diffusion matrix obtained by optimizing first and then taking the homogenized limit coincides with the optimal diffusion matrix for the homogenized problem, therefore proving that maximizing an effective diffusion is equivalent to optimizing a spectral gap on the periodized problem before homogenizing. The second approach, namely optimizing the homogenized problem, is more practical, and this is the main motivation for the commutation result we establish. Lastly, we present in
Section

5.4
numerical experiments that confirm our theoretical analysis.

5.1
Definitions and compactness results

We present in this section compactness results for sequences of matrices
(\mathcal{A}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
. Let us emphasize that we restrict ourselves to
a,b>0
in all this section. We start by recalling the notion of
H
-convergence in the setting of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic functions. Compared to the classical
H
-convergence presented in the literature (see for instance
[
2
, Chapter 1]
and references therein, as well as
[
14
]
), the main difference is that we use periodic boundary conditions rather than Dirichlet boundary conditions.

Definition 1

(
H
-convergence)
.

Fix
a,b>0
, and consider a sequence
(\mathcal{A}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
. The sequence
(\mathcal{A}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
is said to
H
-converge to
\overline{\mathcal{A}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
(denoted by
\mathcal{A}^{k}\xrightarrow{H}\overline{\mathcal{A}}
) if, for any
f\in H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that
\langle f,\mathbf{1}\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=0
, the sequence
(u^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
of solutions to

\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\div\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla u^{k}\right)=f&\text{ on }\mathbb{T}^{d},\\
&\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{k}(q)\,dq=0,&\end{aligned}\right.

(36)

satisfies, in the limit
k\to+\infty
,

\left\{\begin{aligned} u^{k}&\rightharpoonup u\text{ weakly in }H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\\
\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla u^{k}&\rightharpoonup\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla u\text{ weakly in }L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d},\end{aligned}\right.

where
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is the solution of the homogenized problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\div\left(\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla u\right)=f&\text{ on }\mathbb{T}^{d},\\
&\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(q)\,dq=0.&\end{aligned}\right.

(37)

Notice that the functions
u^{k}
in (
36
) and
u
in (
37
) are indeed well-defined since
\langle f,\mathbf{1}\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=0
(in application of the Lax–Milgram theorem, see
Lemma

27
in
Appendix

D
below).
The next theorem, proved for completeness in
Section

D.1
, states a compactness result for sequences of periodic functions.

Theorem 6

(Murat–Tartar)
.

Fix
a,b>0
, and consider a sequence
(\mathcal{A}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
. Up to extraction of a subsequence,
(\mathcal{A}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}

H
-converges to a matrix
\overline{\mathcal{A}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
(non constant in general).

The following theorem, adapted from
[
2
, Theorem 1.3.18]
(and proved for completeness in
Section

D.4.1
, see
Remark

12
), states an
H
-convergence result for sequences of matrices defined through periodic homogenization. It is a particular case of
Theorem

6
specific to periodic functions.

Theorem 7
.

Fix
a,b>0
, and let
\mathcal{A}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
. For
k\geqslant 1
, consider the sequence
(\mathcal{A}_{\#,k})_{k\geqslant 1}
defined as

\forall q\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\qquad\mathcal{A}_{\#,k}(q)=\mathcal{A}(kq).

The sequence
(\mathcal{A}_{\#,k})_{k\geqslant 1}

H
-converges to the constant matrix
\overline{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathcal{M}_{a,b}
with entries

\forall 1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d,\qquad\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{ij}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(e_{i}+\nabla w_{i}(q))^{\top}\mathcal{A}(q)(e_{j}+\nabla w_{j}(q))\,dq,

(38)

where
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{d})
is the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\{w_{i}\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant d}\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is the family of unique solutions to the problems

\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\div\left(\mathcal{A}(e_{i}+\nabla w_{i})\right)=0,\\
&\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}w_{i}(q)\,dq=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(39)

The last theorem, proved in
Section

D.2
by a straightforward adaptation of
[
2
, Theorem 1.3.16]
, states that
H
-convergence implies the convergence of the sequence of spectral gaps.

Theorem 8
.

Fix
a,b>0
and let
(\mathcal{A}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
be a sequence
H
-converging to a homogenized matrix
\overline{\mathcal{A}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
. Let
(\rho^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
be a sequence of positive functions converging weakly-
*
in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to a limiting function
\rho
, and such that there exists
\rho_{-},\rho_{+}>0
for which
0<\rho_{-}\leqslant\rho^{k}(q)\leqslant\rho_{+}<+\infty
for all
k\geqslant 1
and for almost all
q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Let
\lambda^{k}
be the smallest nonzero eigenvalue and
u^{k}
an associated normalized eigenvector of the spectral problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\div\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}(q)\nabla u^{k}(q)\right)=\lambda^{k}\rho^{k}(q)u^{k}(q),\\
&\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{k}(q)^{2}\,dq=1.\end{aligned}\right.

Then,

\lim_{k\rightarrow+\infty}\lambda^{k}=\overline{\lambda}

and, up to a subsequence,
(u^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
converges weakly in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to
u
,
where
(\overline{\lambda},u)
is an eigenpair of the homogenized operator:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\div\left(\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla u(q)\right)=\overline{\lambda}\rho(q)u(q),\\
&\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(q)^{2}\,dq=1,\end{aligned}\right.

and
\overline{\lambda}
is the smallest nonzero eigenvalue of this homogenized eigenvalue problem.

5.2
Optimization of the periodic homogenization limit

We still fix
a,b>0
, and apply the results of
Section

5.1
to a particular class of sequences of matrix valued-functions
(\mathcal{A}_{\#,k})_{k\geqslant 1}
, defined for
\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
by decreasing the period of the
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic matrix

\mathcal{A}(q)=\mathcal{D}(q)\exp(-V(q))

(40)

to obtain the
(\mathbb{Z}/k)^{d}
-periodic function

\mathcal{A}_{\#,k}(q)=\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}(q)\exp\left(-V_{\#,k}(q)\right),\qquad\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}(q)=\mathcal{D}(kq),\qquad V_{\#,k}(q)=V(kq).

(41)

Note that
\mathcal{A}_{\#,k}(q)\in\mathcal{M}_{a,b}
for almost every
q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
and for all
k\geqslant 1
.
The periodization procedure (
41
) allows us to prove in
Section

5.2.1
the
H
-convergence of the sequence of matrices, and the convergence of the associated eigenvalues and eigenvectors (see
Corollary

9
). We then provide in
Section

5.2.2
explicit expressions for diffusion matrices
\mathcal{D}
which maximize the spectral gap of the homogenized problem; see in particular
Proposition

11
for the one dimensional case
d=1
.

5.2.1
Periodic homogenization of the spectral gap problem

We consider the process
(q^{k}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
defined on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
by the dynamics

dq^{k}_{t}=\left[-\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}(q^{k}_{t})\nabla V_{\#,k}(q^{k}_{t})+\div(\mathcal{D}_{\#,k})(q^{k}_{t})\right]dt+\sqrt{2}\,\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}^{1/2}(q^{k}_{t})\,dW_{t}.

(42)

The generator associated to the dynamics acts on test functions
u:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
as

\mathcal{L}_{k}u=\exp\left(V_{\#,k}\right)\div\left[\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}\exp\left(-V_{\#,k}\right)\nabla u\right].

Since the eigenfunctions associated with the first eigenvalue 0 are constant functions, the Rayleigh–Ritz principle implies that the spectral gap of
\mathcal{L}_{k}
considered as an operator on
L^{2}(Z^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\#,k}})
(recall that
Z
is defined in (
1
)) writes (recalling the notation (
41
)):

\Lambda_{\#,k}(\mathcal{D})=\min_{u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\setminus\{0\}}\left\{\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u(q)^{\top}\mathcal{A}_{\#,k}(q)\nabla u(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{2}(q)\,\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\#,k}(q)}\,dq}\ \middle|\ \int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u\,\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\#,k}}=0\right\}.

(43)

Let us emphasize that the test functions to find the minimum on the right-hand side of the previous equality are
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic, while the diffusion and the potential are
(\mathbb{Z}/k)^{d}
-periodic. Note also that the minimization over the functions
u
is performed on
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, which is equivalent in fact to the functional space
H^{1}(Z^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\#,k}})
since
\mathbb{T}^{d}
is compact and
V
is smooth. This allows to work with a fixed functional space for the test functions
u
whatever the value of
k
.

The spectral gap
\Lambda_{\#,k}(\mathcal{D})
is the smallest nonzero solution
\lambda^{k}
of the following eigenvalue problem posed on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
: find
(u^{k},\lambda^{k})\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\setminus\left\{0\right\}\times(0,+\infty)
such that

-\div\left[\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}\exp\left(-V_{\#,k}\right)\nabla u^{k}\right]=\lambda^{k}\exp\left(-V_{\#,k}\right)u^{k},\qquad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{k}\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\#,k}}=0.

By applying
Theorem

8
to the sequence
(\mathcal{A}_{\#,k})_{k\geqslant 1}
, we therefore obtain the following result on the convergence of the sequence of spectral gaps
\left(\Lambda_{\#,k}(\mathcal{D})\right)_{k\geqslant 1}
for a given diffusion
\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
.

Corollary 9
.

Fix
p\in[1,+\infty)
,
a,b>0
satisfying (
17
) and
\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
. Then,
\mathcal{A}_{\#,k}\xrightarrow{H}\overline{\mathcal{A}}
with
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
defined in (
38
). Defining
\overline{\mathcal{D}}=Z^{-1}\overline{\mathcal{A}}
, the sequence
\left(\Lambda_{\#,k}(\mathcal{D})\right)_{k\geqslant 1}
converges as
k\to+\infty
towards

\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{D}):=\inf_{u\in H^{1,0}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u(q)^{\top}\overline{\mathcal{D}}\nabla u(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{2}(q)\,dq}.

Remark 7
.

Consider the process
(q_{t}^{k})_{t\geqslant 0}
defined in (
42
), and define the rescaled process
\left(Q_{t}^{k}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}=\left(k^{-1}q^{k}_{k^{2}t}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
for
k\geqslant 1
. By adapting the arguments from
[
30
, Theorem 1]
(see also
[
11
, Chapter 3]
), this rescaled process can be shown to converge, in the limit
k\to+\infty
, to an effective Brownian motion on the
d
-dimensional torus whose diffusion is the homogenized diffusion
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
introduced in Corollary
9
.

In the next section, we seek to maximize the spectral gap
\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{D})
with respect to the baseline diffusion matrix
\mathcal{D}
from which
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
arises as the
H
-limit of
\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}(q)\exp(- V_{\#,k}(q))/Z
. There exists a large body of literature on optimizing homogenized coefficients, for example in the context of shape optimization of materials, typically by considering laminated structures which would correspond here to situations where the diffusion can only take two values; see for instance
[
73
,
39
,
9
,
4
]
and
[
42
, Chapter 10]
.

It is useful to provide a more explicit expression of the limiting matrix
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
in order to maximize
\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{D})
. Consider
\xi=\xi_{1}e_{1}+\dots+\xi_{d}e_{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and note that

\xi^{\top}\overline{\mathcal{D}}\xi=Z^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(\xi+\nabla w_{\xi}(q))^{\top}\mathcal{A}(q)(\xi+\nabla w_{\xi}(q))\,dq,

where
w_{\xi}=\xi_{1}w_{1}+\dots+\xi_{d}w_{d}
and the family
(w_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant d}
is defined in (
39
). Recalling (
40
), it follows that

\displaystyle\xi^{\top}\overline{\mathcal{D}}\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left(\xi+\nabla w_{\xi}(q)\right)^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)\left(\xi+\nabla w_{\xi}(q)\right)\,\mu(q)\,dq

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left(\xi^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)\xi+2\xi^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)\nabla w_{\xi}(q)+\nabla w_{\xi}(q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)\nabla w_{\xi}(q)\right)\mu(q)\,dq.

(44)

By the weak formulation of (
39
), it holds, for any
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\xi^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)\nabla v(q)\,\mu(q)\,dq=-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla w_{\xi}(q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)\nabla v(q)\,\mu(q)\,dq.

(45)

Upon replacing
v
by
w_{\xi}
, we obtain with (
44
) that

\xi^{\top}\overline{\mathcal{D}}\xi=\xi^{\top}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{D}(q)\,\mu(q)\,dq\right)\xi-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla w_{\xi}(q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)\nabla w_{\xi}(q)\,\mu(q)\,dq.

(46)

5.2.2
Optimization of the homogenized limit

We now focus on the optimization with respect to
\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
of the homogenized limit
\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}({\mathcal{D}})
given in
Corollary

9
, under some normalization constraint:

\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}^{\star}=\sup_{\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{D}).

(47)

This optimization problem is well posed by arguments similar to the ones used in
Section

3.1
, and using the fact that the mapping
\mathcal{D}\mapsto\overline{\mathcal{D}}
in (
46
) is continuous on
\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
(relying on the continuity of the mapping
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})\ni\mathcal{A}\mapsto w_{i}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
where
w_{i}
is the solution of (
39
)). In the most general case, one of the difficulties is to understand how the constraint
\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
homogenizes into a constraint on
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
. We start by analyzing a specific example of constrained set with linear constraints, which is not the constraint
\|\mathcal{D}\|_{L^{1}_{V}}\leqslant 1
with the norm defined in (
12
). The interest of the linear constraint we consider is that the optimization problem becomes trivial. We next derive a similar result for the constraint
\|\mathcal{D}\|_{L^{p}_{V}}\leqslant 1
, but for the one-dimensional case
d=1
. Both results hold provided
a,b>0
are sufficiently small so that the optimal diffusion matrix automatically satisfies the pointwise lower and upper bounds involving
a
and
b
.

Linear constraint.

We start by considering the following constraint on
\mathcal{D}
, for a given constant matrix
M\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{++}
(with
\mathcal{S}_{d}^{++}
the set of real, symmetric, positive, definite matrices of sizes
d\times d
):

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{D}(q)\,\mu(q)\,dq=M.

(48)

Note that this imposes
d(d+1)/2
scalar constraints on
\mathcal{D}
instead of only 1 scalar constraint when considering the
L^{p}_{V}
constraint (
13
). This is the only setting where we are able to provide an explicit formula for the homogenized optimization problem in dimension
d>1
.

Proposition 10
.

Consider
M\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{++}
, with
M_{-},M_{+}>0
such that
M_{-}\mathrm{Id}_{d}\leqslant M\leqslant M_{+}^{-1}\mathrm{Id}_{d}
. Fix
a,b>0
such that

0<a\leqslant M_{-}Z,\qquad 0<b\leqslant\frac{1}{M_{+}Z}.

(49)

Then, the diffusion
\mathcal{D}^{\star}_{\mathrm{hom}}(q)=M/\mu(q)
is a solution to the optimization problem

\max\left\{\Lambda_{\rm{hom}}(\mathcal{D})\,\middle|\,\mathcal{D}\in L_{V}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b}),\ \ \int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{D}(q)\,\mu(q)\,dq=M\right\}.

Proof.

In view of (
46
), for any
\mathcal{D}\in L_{V}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
satisfying (
48
), it holds

\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\qquad\xi^{\top}\overline{\mathcal{D}}\xi\leqslant\xi^{\top}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{D}(q)\,\mu(q)\,dq\right)\xi=\xi^{\top}M\xi.

Thus, for any
\mathcal{D}\in L_{V}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
satisfying the constraint (
48
),

\inf_{u\in H^{1,0}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u(q)^{\top}\overline{\mathcal{D}}\nabla u(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{2}(q)\,dq}\leqslant\inf_{u\in H^{1,0}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u(q)^{\top}M\nabla u(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{2}(q)\,dq}.

Now, note that
\mathcal{D}(q)=M/\mu(q)
belongs to
L_{V}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
in view of the choice (
49
), and satisfies
\overline{\mathcal{D}}=M
in view of (
46
) since
w_{\xi}=0
for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by (
39
). This diffusion matrix therefore maximizes the limiting spectral gap in view of the above inequality, which concludes the proof.
∎

L^{p}_{V}
constraint in the one-dimensional case.

We now restrict ourselves to the case where
d=1
. In this situation,
\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{D})
is proportional to the nonnegative real number
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
. Thus, maximizing
\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{D})
boils down to maximizing
\overline{\mathcal{D}}\geqslant 0
. In the one-dimensional case, denoting
w_{\xi}
as
w_{\mathcal{D}}
(we omit the irrelevant parameter
\xi
, but make the dependence on
\mathcal{D}
explicit), equation (
39
) on
w_{\mathcal{D}}
can be rewritten as

\left[\mathrm{e}^{-V}\mathcal{D}(1+w_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime})\right]^{\prime}=0,

(50)

and the expression (
46
) simplifies as

\overline{\mathcal{D}}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{D}(q)\left(1-w^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}(q)^{2}\right)\mu(q)\,dq,

while the normalization constraint
\|\mathcal{D}\|_{L^{p}_{V}}\leqslant 1
simply reads

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{D}(q)^{p}\mathrm{e}^{-pV(q)}\,dq\leqslant 1.

The optimization problem (
47
) is therefore equivalent to

\max_{\mathcal{D}\in L^{p}_{V}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}_{+})}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{D}(q)\left(1-w^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}(q)^{2}\right)\mu(q)\,dq\ \middle|\ a\leqslant\mathcal{D}\mathrm{e}^{-V}\leqslant b^{-1},\ \int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{D}(q)^{p}\,\mathrm{e}^{-pV(q)}\,dq\leqslant 1\right\}.

This optimization problem admits an explicit solution provided
a,b\leqslant 1
, as made precise in the following result.

Proposition 11
.

Let
d=1
. Fix
p\in[1,+\infty)
, and consider
0<a\leqslant 1\leqslant b^{-1}<+\infty
. Then, a maximizer for the spectral gap in the homogenized limit (
47
) is

\mathcal{D}^{\star}_{\mathrm{hom}}(q)=\mathrm{e}^{V(q)}.

(51)

Note that the maximizer
\mathcal{D}^{\star}_{\mathrm{hom}}
is independent of
p
due to our specific choice of normalization, and that no extra normalization factor is needed.

Proof.

Equation (
50
) can be integrated as
w_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}+1=K\mathcal{D}^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{V}
for some constant
K\in\mathbb{R}
, so that (up to an irrelevant additive constant)

w_{\mathcal{D}}(q)=-q+K\int_{0}^{q}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{V}}{\mathcal{D}}.

The constant
K
is chosen to ensure the periodicity of the function. This gives

w_{\mathcal{D}}(q)=-q+\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{V}}{\mathcal{D}}\right)^{-1}\int_{0}^{q}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{V}}{\mathcal{D}},

so that, by (
46
) and (
45
) (with
\xi=1
in this one-dimensional context, and
v=w_{\xi}=w_{\mathcal{D}}
),

\overline{\mathcal{D}}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{D}\left[1-\left(w_{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}\right)^{2}\right]\mu=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{D}\left(1+w^{\prime}_{\mathcal{D}}\right)\mu=\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathrm{e}^{-V}\right)^{-1}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{D}^{-1}\,\mathrm{e}^{V}\right)^{-1}.

(52)

The maximization of the effective diffusion
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
with respect to
\mathcal{D}
therefore amounts to solving the following optimization problem:

\min\left\{\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{D}^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{V}\ \middle|\ a\leqslant\mathcal{D}\mathrm{e}^{-V}\leqslant b^{-1},\ \int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{D}(q)^{p}\,\mathrm{e}^{-pV(q)}\,dq\leqslant 1\right\}.

The Euler–Lagrange equation associated with the minimization problem with the normalization constraint only (forgetting the pointwise upper and lower bounds), assuming that the constraint is saturated at its value 1, implies that
\mathcal{D}^{-2}\mathrm{e}^{V}
is proportional to
\mathcal{D}^{p-1}\mathrm{e}^{-pV}
,
i.e.

\mathcal{D}
is proportional to
\mathrm{e}^{V}
; and in fact equal to the latter function in view of the normalization constraint. The pointwise upper and lower bounds are then automatically satisfied given our choice for
a,b
, which allows to conclude the proof.
∎

The optimal diffusion in the homogenized limit (
51
) therefore modulates the speed of the exploration of the potential energy surface based on the configuration’s energy level: the system slows down in important regions of the configuration space, where
V
is smaller, and tends to quickly cross low probability regions, where
V
is larger. Intuitively, this favors transitions between high-probability regions (modes of the distribution, corresponding to low energy regions).

The extension of this result to the multi-dimensional case
d\geqslant 1
is not as clear as computations cannot be easily performed analytically and we leave this open question for future work.

5.3
Homogenization of the optimal diffusion

We consider in this section the
H
-limit of diffusion matrices which maximize the spectral gap for potentials with decreasing spatial periodicity. This corresponds to first performing the optimization of the spectral gap, then the homogenization of the associated maximizers; whereas the analysis of
Section

5.2.1
relied on first homogenizing the spectral gap problem, then optimizing it in
Section

5.2.2
.

For
k\geqslant 1
, define the constrained set associated with the oscillating potential
V_{\#,k}
:

\mathfrak{D}_{\#,k,p}^{a,b}=\left\{\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}_{V_{\#,k}}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})\,\middle|\,\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left|\mathcal{D}(q)\right|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p}\,\mathrm{e}^{-pV_{\#,k}(q)}\,dq\leqslant 1\right\}.

Note that, if
\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
, then
\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}\in\mathfrak{D}_{\#,k,p}^{a,b}
, where we recall that
\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}
is the periodized diffusion
\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}(q)=\mathcal{D}(kq)
. Of course, the set
\mathfrak{D}_{\#,k,p}^{a,b}
contains more functions than those obtained by periodization. Let us indeed emphasize that the diffusion matrices in
\mathfrak{D}_{\#,k,p}^{a,b}
are a priori only
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic. For
\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{\#,k,p}^{a,b}
a
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic diffusion, consider the spectral gap associated with the oscillating potential
V_{\#,k}
:

\Lambda^{k}(\mathcal{D})=\min_{u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\setminus\{0\}}\left\{\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u(q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)\nabla u(q)\,\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\#,k}(q)}\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{2}(q)\,\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\#,k}(q)}\,dq}\ \middle|\ \int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u\,\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\#,k}}=0\right\}.

(53)

The optimal diffusion matrix corresponding to (
53
) satisfies the following optimization problem:

\Lambda^{k,\star}=\max_{\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{\#,k,p}^{a,b}}\Lambda^{k}(\mathcal{D}).

(54)

Note that the minimization problem in (
53
) is different from the one in (
43
) since the diffusion matrix
\mathcal{D}
is not
(\mathbb{Z}/k)^{d}
-periodic, but only
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic a priori. It turns out however that the optimization problem (
54
) admits a
(\mathbb{Z}/k)^{d}
-periodic solution, as made precise in the following proposition, proved in
Section

D.3
.

Proposition 12
.

Fix
p\in[1,+\infty)
and consider
a\in[0,a_{\mathrm{max}}]
and
b>0
such that
ab\leqslant 1
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, there exists
\mathcal{D}^{k,\star}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
such that, denoting by
\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}^{k,\star}(q)=\mathcal{D}^{k,\star}(kq)
,

\Lambda^{k}(\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}^{k,\star})=\Lambda_{\#,k}(\mathcal{D}^{k,\star})=\Lambda^{k,\star}.

Proposition

12
shows that, while solving the optimization problem (
54
) (well posed in application of
Theorem

4
), we may restrict ourselves to
(\mathbb{Z}/k)^{d}
-periodic diffusion matrices.
Theorem

13
below, proved in
Section

D.4
, states that the limit
\overline{\Lambda}^{\star}
when
k\to+\infty
of the sequence
(\Lambda^{k,\star})_{k\geqslant 1}
is in fact equal to the limit
\Lambda^{\star}_{\mathrm{hom}}
defined in (
47
), showing the commutation of homogenization and optimization procedures.

Theorem 13
.

Fix
p\in[1,+\infty)
and consider
a\in(0,a_{\mathrm{max}}]
and
b>0
such that
ab\leqslant 1
.
Then the sequence
\left(\Lambda^{k,\star}\right)_{k\geqslant 1}
converges to
\Lambda^{\star}_{\mathrm{hom}}
as
k\to+\infty
.

Theorem

13
suggests that, in the homogenized limit, the optimal diffusion matrix yields the same spectral gap as its proxy obtained by solving (
47
) – which, we recall, can be analytically written out in the simple cases discussed in
Section

5.2.2
.

5.4
Numerical results

We present two one-dimensional numerical examples illustrating the convergence towards the homogenized regime when the frequency
k
goes to infinity. The space discretizations of the optimization problem (
54
), the optimization algorithms, the notation and the hyperparameters are the same as in
Section

4
. The mesh size used to obtain these results is refined as
k
increases, using
N=200k
. Denote by
D^{k,\star}
the maximizer obtained by solving the approximation of the optimal spectral gap (
54
) for a given frequency
k\geqslant 1
and by
\sigma_{2}(D^{k,\star})
the corresponding optimal value. The optimal homogenized coefficient is
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\star}_{\rm hom}=1/Z
, which is easily obtained from the formula (
52
) together with the expression of the optimal diffusion coefficient in the homogenized limit (
51
). Therefore, the value of the homogenized limit of the spectral gap is the product of the first non-zero eigenvalue of
-\Delta
on
\mathbb{T}
with
\overline{\mathcal{D}}^{\star}_{\rm hom}
, which yields
\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}^{\star}=4\pi^{2}/Z
.

The convergence of
D^{k,\star}
towards
D^{\star}_{\rm hom}
is illustrated in
Figure

6
. In particular, we represent on the upper right and left plots the optimal diffusion coefficients
D^{k,\star}
for increasing values of
k
, along with the diffusion coefficient
D^{\star}_{\mathrm{hom}}
. Note that we always observe that the diffusion
D^{k,\star}
is
1/k
-periodic, and we only plot its trace on
[0,1/k]
(and stretch it on
[0,1]
) so that optimal diffusion coefficients for various values of
k
can be compared: more precisely, the function plotted for the optimal diffusion coefficient for the frequency
k\geqslant 1
is
q\mapsto D^{k,\star}_{\left\lfloor\frac{Nq}{k}\right\rfloor+1}
.

We observe that, on these simple one-dimensional examples, the optimal and homogenized diffusion coefficients coincide almost perfectly for relatively small values of the frequency, namely already for
k=3
. We also observe that the optimal diffusion coefficient seems to vanish at two points for
k=1
(and for
k=2
for the numerical example presented in
Figure

6(a)
), whereas it is uniformly positive definite for
k\geqslant 3
.

(a)

Potential:
V(q)=\sin(4\pi q)(2+\sin(2\pi q))
.

(b)

Potential:
V(q)=\cos(4\pi q)
.

Figure 6
:
Comparison of the two procedures: homogenize then optimize (leading to the solid blue line ‘homogenized diffusion’), versus optimize then homogenize (series of curves indexed by increasing values of
k
). The left and right columns correspond to two different target distributions. The figures in the second row illustrate the convergence of the sequence
\left(\sigma_{2}(D^{k,\star})\right)_{k\geqslant 1}
towards
\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}^{\star}
.

6
Application to sampling algorithms

We present in this section an application of the optimization procedure to accelerate sampling algorithms. We focus on the Random Walk Metropolis–Hastings (RWMH) algorithm
[
58
,
40
]
to unbiasedly sample from a Boltzmann–Gibbs target distribution. As we show in
Section

6.1
, this algorithm provides a consistent discretization of the continuous dynamics (
3
). More precisely, we show that the RWMH with proposals constructed using a space dependent diffusion converges, in the limit
\Delta t\to 0
, to a Langevin diffusion with space dependent diffusion given by the proposal variance. Notice that we do not discuss the ergodicity of such dynamics, see
[
56
]
for results in that direction. We next present in
Section

6.2
numerical experiments demonstrating that optimizing the diffusion leads to a more efficient sampling of the target measure.
We numerically compare three variants of RWMH corresponding to three different choices for the variance of the proposal. The first variant is a simple RWMH algorithm with constant proposal variance; the two others have a space-dependent proposal variance
\Delta t\,\mathcal{D}
, with
\mathcal{D}
given by (i) the pre-computed optimal diffusion matrix and (ii) the explicit homogenized limit of
Section

5.2.2
.

Remark 8

(On the choice of the dynamics)
.

We consider a simple Random-Walk algorithm with moves of size
\mathrm{O}(\sqrt{\Delta t})
rather than a Metropolization of a Euler–Maruyama discretization of the overdamped Langevin dynamics (the SmartMC method
[
71
]
in molecular dynamics, known as MALA
[
70
]
in computational statistics). Indeed, the latter dynamics have rejection probabilities of order
\Delta t^{3/2}
for constant diffusion matrices, but a much larger rejection probability of order
\sqrt{\Delta t}
for genuinely position dependent diffusion matrices
[
31
]
(see also
Lemma

34
in
Section

E.3
below). In contrast, the rejection probability is of order
\sqrt{\Delta t}
in all cases for Random Walk Metropolis–Hastings. We therefore choose RWMH in order to have algorithms with rejection probabilities of the same order of magnitude, which allows for a fairer comparison of the dynamics. Let us mention that numerical schemes leading to a rejection probability scaling as
\Delta t^{3/2}
for Metropolizations of overdamped Langevin dynamics with position dependent diffusion matrices have recently been proposed in
[
52
]
. We also refer to
[
62
]
for discussions about efficient discretizations of the overdamped Langevin dynamics with position dependent diffusions, using time-rescaling transforms.

6.1
Consistency of the Random Walk Metropolis–Hastings algorithm

We describe in this section the RWMH algorithm we consider, and then provide consistency results of the method in the limit
\Delta t\to 0
.

Description of the Metropolis algorithm.

The Random Walk Metropolis–Hastings algorithm
[
58
,
40
]
is obtained by first proposing moves constructed from the current configuration by adding a Gaussian increment, and then accepting or rejecting the proposal move according to a Metropolis criterion. More precisely, starting from the current configuration
q^{i}_{\Delta t}
for
i\geqslant 0
and using a discretization time step
\Delta t>0
, a new configuration is proposed as

\widetilde{q}^{i+1}_{\Delta t}=q^{i}_{\Delta t}+\sqrt{2\Delta t}\,\mathcal{D}^{1/2}(q^{i}_{\Delta t})\,G^{i+1},

(55)

where
(G^{i})_{i\geqslant 1}
is a sequence of independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) normal random variables. The transition kernel
\mathscr{T}(q^{i}_{\Delta t},q^{i+1}_{\Delta t})
associated with this transition has density

\mathscr{T}(q,q^{\prime})=\left(\frac{1}{4\pi\Delta t}\right)^{d/2}\det\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4\Delta t}(q^{\prime}-q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1}(q^{\prime}-q)\right).

Note that the reverse move associated with the proposal (
55
) is

q^{i}_{\Delta t}=\widetilde{q}^{i+1}_{\Delta t}-\sqrt{2\Delta t}\,\mathcal{D}^{1/2}(\widetilde{q}^{i+1}_{\Delta t})\,\widetilde{G}^{i+1},\qquad\widetilde{G}^{i+1}=\mathcal{D}^{-1/2}(\widetilde{q}^{i+1}_{\Delta t})\mathcal{D}^{1/2}(q^{i}_{\Delta t})\,G^{i+1}.

This allows to compute the Metropolis ratio as

\displaystyle R_{\Delta t}(q^{i}_{\Delta t},G^{i+1})

\displaystyle=\min\left\{1,\frac{\mu(\widetilde{q}^{i+1}_{\Delta t})\mathscr{T}(\widetilde{q}^{i+1}_{\Delta t},q^{i}_{\Delta t})}{\mu(q^{i}_{\Delta t})\mathscr{T}(q^{i}_{\Delta t},\widetilde{q}^{i+1}_{\Delta t})}\right\}

(56)

\displaystyle=\min\left\{1,\left(\frac{\det\mathcal{D}(q^{i}_{\Delta t})}{\det\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{q}^{i+1}_{\Delta t})}\right)^{1/2}\mathrm{e}^{-[V(\widetilde{q}^{i+1}_{\Delta t})-V(q^{i}_{\Delta t})]-(|\widetilde{G}^{i+1}|^{2}-|G^{i+1}|^{2})/2}\right\}.

Accepting the proposal with probability
R_{\Delta t}(q^{i}_{\Delta t},G^{i+1})
using a sequence of i.i.d. random variables
(U^{i})_{i\geqslant 1}
with uniform law on
[0,1]
, independent of
(G^{i})_{i\geqslant 1}
, then leads to setting the new configuration as

q^{i+1}_{\Delta t}=q^{i}_{\Delta t}+\sqrt{2\Delta t}\,\mathcal{D}^{1/2}(q^{i}_{\Delta t})\,G^{i+1}\mathbbm{1}_{\{U^{i+1}\leqslant R^{n}(q^{i}_{\Delta t},G^{i+1})\}}.

(57)

Weak error estimates and pathwise weak convergence.

The following result, similar to
[
31
, Lemma 4]
, implies that the numerical scheme (
57
) is weakly consistent. The proof is omitted since it exactly follows the proof of
[
31
, Lemma 4]
as a particular case when the drift term is 0.

Proposition 14
.

For any
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, there exists
K\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\Delta t^{\ast}>0
such that, for any
0<\Delta t\leqslant\Delta t^{\ast}
,

\sup_{q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left\lvert\frac{\mathbb{E}^{q}\left(\varphi(q_{\Delta t}^{1})\right)-\varphi(q)-\Delta t\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\varphi(q)}{\Delta t^{3/2}},\right\rvert\leqslant K,

where
\mathbb{E}^{q}
denote the expectation with respect to all realizations of the Markov chain (
57
) starting from
q_{\Delta t}^{0}=q
.

We next state a weak consistency result of the Metropolis scheme with the baseline continuous dynamics (
3
) at the level of the paths of the process. Define the rescaled, linearly interpolated continuous-time process
(Q^{\Delta t}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
by

Q^{\Delta t}_{t}=\left(\left\lceil\frac{t}{\Delta t}\right\rceil-\frac{t}{\Delta t}\right)q_{\Delta t}^{\left\lfloor\frac{t}{\Delta t}\right\rfloor}+\left(\frac{t}{\Delta t}-\left\lfloor\frac{t}{\Delta t}\right\rfloor\right)q_{\Delta t}^{\left\lceil\frac{t}{\Delta t}\right\rceil},

(58)

with
Q_{0}^{\Delta t}=q_{0}
given. We can then state the following pathwise consistency result, proved in
Appendix

E
.

Theorem 15
.

Assume that
\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{S}_{d}^{++})
, and denote by
P_{\Delta t}
the law of the process
(Q^{\Delta t}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
on
\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,+\infty),\mathbb{T}^{d})
, starting from a given initial condition
Q_{0}^{\Delta t}=q_{0}
. Then,
P_{\Delta t}
converges weakly to
P
as
\Delta t\to 0
, where
P
is the law of the unique solution to the stochastic differential equation

dQ_{t}=\left(-\mathcal{D}(Q_{t})\nabla V(Q_{t})+\div\mathcal{D}(Q_{t})\right)dt+\sqrt{2}\,\mathcal{D}^{1/2}(Q_{t})\,dW_{t},

(59)

where
(W_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
is a standard
d
-dimensional Brownian motion, with initial condition
Q_{0}=q_{0}
.

Note that we assume that
\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathcal{S}_{d}^{++})
for some technical estimates, in particular those of
Lemma

34
in
Section

E.3
below.

6.2
Sampling experiments

In this section, we illustrate the benefits of using a position dependent diffusion coefficient for sampling a Boltzmann–Gibbs target distribution. We consider a linear interpolation of the optimal diffusion coefficient
D^{\star}
(introduced in
Section

4
) obtained with
N=I=1000
and
a=b=0
, the diffusion coefficient for the homogenized problem
D^{\star}_{\mathrm{hom}}
(see
Section

5
and equation (
51
)), and the constant diffusion coefficient
D_{\rm cst}
(see
Section

4
).

We choose the one-dimensional double well potential
V(q)=\sin(4\pi q)(2+\sin(2\pi q))
defined on
\mathbb{T}
(the associated Gibbs distribution is displayed in
Figure

4(a)
). The potential exhibits a global minimum on
\mathbb{T}
at
x_{0}\approx 0.36544
and a local minimum at
x_{1}\approx 0.89714
.

Sample trajectories.

We show in
Figure

7
sample trajectories following the discretization (
57
) for each diffusion coefficient. In order to compare the simulations, the same Gaussian variables
(G^{i})_{i\geqslant 1}
are used in (
55
). They are run with time step
\Delta t=10^{-4}
for
N_{\rm it}=10^{6}
iterations, with the same initial condition
q_{0}=0
. This time step is chosen so that the rejection probability in the Metropolis–Hastings procedure is of order 5%; see
Table

2
for the values of the rejection probabilities in each case, as well as
Figure

11(b)
for a study of the scaling of the rejection probability as a function of the time step. We observe that the dynamics using a constant diffusion coefficient is stuck longer in wells, whereas the dynamics using either the optimal or homogenized diffusion coefficient transitions faster between metastable states.

Mean Squared Displacement and effective diffusion.

We next compute the mean squared displacement (MSD) averaged over
N_{\mathrm{sim}}=10000
realizations of the dynamics for the various optimal diffusion coefficients. Each realization starts at position
q_{0}=0
, and the physical simulation time is set to
10
units. Therefore, the MSD at iteration
n\geqslant 1
is

(\mathrm{MSD})_{n}=\frac{1}{N_{\mathrm{sim}}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{sim}}}\left\lvert q^{i,n}_{\Delta t}-q_{0}\right\rvert^{2},

where
q_{\Delta t}^{i,n}
is the (unperiodized) position of the
i
-th realization of the dynamics at iteration
n
for a given time step
\Delta t>0
. The results for
\Delta t=10^{-7}
are presented in
Figure

8(a)
. The MSD is computed every
1000
steps. The ribbons represent 95% confidence intervals, quantified using the variance estimated over the
N_{\rm sim}
independent realizations. We observe more displacement when using either the optimal or the homogenized diffusion coefficient, which confirms that the associated dynamics diffuse more over the configuration space than the dynamics using a constant diffusion coefficient.

Observe that the MSD depends linearly on time: this is an illustration of the celebrated Enstein formula, the linear coefficient being twice the
effective diffusion
(we refer for instance to
[
31
]
and references therein for more theoretical details). The effective diffusion is a function of the diffusion coefficient
\mathcal{D}
, and is maximal when choosing the optimal homogenized diffusion
\mathcal{D}_{\rm hom}^{\star}
defined by (
52
). In fact, for this particular choice, the effective diffusion has a simple analytical expression, given by
1/Z\approx 0.375
where
Z
is the normalization constant introduced in (
1
). In
Figure

8(b)
, we compute approximations of the effective diffusion: we run MSD computations for various time steps
\Delta t\in\left\{10^{-7},10^{-6},\dots,10^{-1}\right\}
, and estimate the effective diffusion using a linear regression on the time interval
[5,10]
. These simulations show that we obtain significantly larger MSD whatever the time step
\Delta t
when choosing any of the two optimal diffusions rather than the constant diffusion. The relative positions of the curves stay the same, even for time steps that are not close to the continuous-in-time limit. Lastly, the effective diffusion for the optimal homogenized diffusion for
\Delta t=10^{-7}
is equal to
0.375
, which coincides with the reference value
1/Z
for the continuous-in-time dynamics. We checked that these results were stable when increasing the simulation time up to
100
units.

(a)

Constant diffusion coefficient.

(b)

Optimal diffusion coefficient,
a=0
.

(c)

Homogenized diffusion coefficient.

Figure 7
:
Sample trajectories of the RWMH algorithm for a periodic, one-dimensional potential with double well
V(q)=\sin(4\pi q)(2+\sin(2\pi q))
. Notice that we plot the trajectories over the unfolded configuration space
\mathbb{R}
instead of the torus
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. The two wells corresponding to
x_{0}\approx 0.36544
and
x_{1}\approx 0.89714
are indicated using dashed and dotted horizontal lines in each periodic cell.

(a)

Mean Squared Displacement over time.

(b)

Effective diffusion for various time steps
\Delta t
.

:

Convergence of the empirical law towards the Gibbs measure.

In order to further compare the various diffusion coefficients and their respective effects on the sampling efficiency, we numerically compute the weighted
L^{2}
error (
\chi^{2}
divergence) between the empirical distribution of the discretized process and the Gibbs measure, see (
60
) below. As pointed out in (
5
), for the limiting continuous-in-time dynamics (
59
), this error converges exponentially fast towards 0, at a rate given by the spectral gap of the generator of the dynamics.

More precisely, we set
\Delta t=10^{-6}
and consider an initial distribution
\mu^{0}
made of
N_{\rm samples}=10^{5}
samples generated independently and uniformly over
[0,1]
:

\mu^{0}=\frac{1}{N_{\rm samples}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\rm samples}}\delta_{q_{\Delta t}^{i,0}},\qquad q_{\Delta t}^{i,0}\sim\mathcal{U}([0,1]).

Each sample is then independently updated using the RWMH algorithm presented in
Section

6.1
, so that the empirical distribution
\mu^{n}
at time
n\Delta t
is

\mu^{n}=\frac{1}{N_{\rm samples}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\rm samples}}\delta_{q_{\Delta t}^{i,n}}.

In practice, the weighted
L^{2}
error appearing in the left-hand side of (
5
) is approximated by discretizing the interval
[0,1)
into
N_{\rm bins}
bins of equal sizes: for
1\leqslant k\leqslant N_{\rm bins}
, we denote by
B_{k}=[(k-1)/N_{\rm bins},k/N_{\rm bins})
the
k
-th bin. The weighted
L^{2}
error is then approximated as

\left(\frac{1}{N_{\rm bins}}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\rm bins}}\frac{\left(\widehat{\mu}_{k}^{n}-\widehat{\mu}_{k}\right)^{2}}{\widehat{\mu}_{k}}\right)^{1/2}

(60)

where
\widehat{\mu}_{k}^{n},\widehat{\mu}_{k}
are proportional to the fraction of samples in the
k
-th bin, and normalized as

\frac{1}{N_{\rm bins}}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\rm bins}}\widehat{\mu}_{k}^{n}=\frac{1}{N_{\rm bins}}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\rm bins}}\widehat{\mu}_{k}=1.

In particular, in our case,

\forall 1\leqslant k\leqslant N_{\rm bins},\qquad\widehat{\mu}_{k}=N_{\rm bins}\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\tfrac{k-1/2}{N_{\rm bins}}\right)}\Big/\sum_{j=1}^{N_{\rm bins}}\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{j-1/2}{N_{\rm bins}}\right)},

approximates the Gibbs distribution, and

\forall 1\leqslant k\leqslant N_{\rm bins},\qquad\widehat{\mu}^{n}_{k}=\frac{N_{\rm bins}}{N_{\rm samples}}\left\lvert\left\{1\leqslant i\leqslant N_{\rm samples},\,q_{\Delta t}^{i,n}\in B_{k}\right\}\right\rvert,

approximates the empirical distribution
\mu^{n}
, where
\left\lvert A\right\rvert
denotes the cardinal of a set
A
. We run the RWMH algorithm for
N_{\rm it}=10^{6}
iterations and use
N_{\rm bins}=100
to approximate the weighted
L^{2}
error.

The error between the empirical distribution and the Gibbs distribution as a function of the physical time is presented in
Figure

9
. This confirms that the use of the optimal diffusion or the homogenized coefficient greatly improves the convergence of the law of the process towards equilibrium. The exponential rate of convergence, corresponding to the dotted lines, is quickly reached. Recall that the spectral gaps for the optimal, homogenized and constant diffusion coefficients are respectively 11.23, 10.58 and 0.81. This is in agreement with the numerical results of
Figure

9
which show a slightly lower error for the optimal diffusion coefficient than for the homogenized diffusion coefficient, and a significantly lower error than for the constant diffusion coefficient.

Figure 9
:
Weighted
L^{2}
error (in logarithmic scale) between the empirical distribution and the Gibbs distribution over time for various diffusion coefficients.

Spatial analysis of the rejection probability of the Metropolis–Hastings procedure.

We compute the average rejection probability of the Metropolis–Hastings procedure for a fixed time step
\Delta t=0.01
. We discretize the torus with a set of points
q^{i}=i/1000
for
0\leqslant i<1000
. For each point
q^{i}
, we propose
10^{5}
moves following the rule (
55
), and count how many proposals are accepted when performing the Metropolis–Hastings procedure,
i.e.
computing (
56
) and comparing its value to a random number uniformly distributed over
[0,1]
.

We represent on
Figure

10
, for various diffusion coefficients, the three following functions: the target distribution, the diffusion coefficient and the average rejection probability. We discarded the error bars as they were too small to be even noticed in the plot.

For the constant diffusion coefficient, for which the results are presented in
Figure

10(a)
, the proposals are most likely rejected when
q
is at the bottom of a well. When using either the optimal diffusion coefficient or the homogenized diffusion coefficient, the behavior is the opposite: the proposals are most likely accepted when
q
is located at the bottom of a well. This is shown in
Figures

10(d)
and
10(b)
. Notice that for the case of the optimal diffusion, higher rejection probabilities are observed where the optimal diffusion coefficient almost vanishes. This is because of the ratio of determinant factors appearing in the denominator of (
56
): even though both terms are of similar orders, they are almost zero, which causes numerical instabilities. We plot in
Figure

10(c)
the average rejection probabilities when using the optimal diffusion coefficients obtained with the optimization procedure and imposing various lower bounds
a\in\left\{0.2,0.4,0.6,0.8,1\right\}
. Of course, using a positive lower bound
a
leads to uniformly positive diffusion coefficients, which is desirable to get stable and ergodic samplers.

(a)

Constant diffusion coefficient.

(b)

Optimal diffusion coefficient,
a=0
.

(c)

Optimal diffusion coefficient: influence of the lower bound
a
on the MH rejection probability.

(d)

Homogenized diffusion coefficient.

Figure 10
:
Spatial analysis of the Metropolis–Hastings rejection probability for the periodic, one-dimensional double well potential
V(q)=\sin(4\pi q)(2+\sin(2\pi q))
.

Transition times.

We compute the mean transition time from the deepest well to any of its two nearby periodized copies. More precisely, we consider the initial condition
q^{0}=x_{0}\approx 0.36544
and compute the average physical time it takes for the system to cross
x_{0}-1
or
x_{0}+1
. This average is computed over
N_{\mathrm{transitions}}=10^{5}
transitions. We show in
Figure

11
the mean transition time with respect to the time step
\Delta t
, as well as the rejection probability of the Metropolis–Hastings procedure. The rejection probability due to the Metropolis–Hastings procedure scales as
\mathrm{O}\left(\sqrt{\Delta t}\right)
; see
[
31
, Section 4.7]
and
Lemma

34
in
Section

E.3
below. We see that the mean transition time between the two metastable states is much smaller when using either the optimal diffusion coefficient or the homogenized diffusion coefficient than with the constant diffusion coefficient. For a time step
\Delta t=5\times 10^{-5}
, the numerical values of the mean transition times when using
D^{\star}
,
D_{\mathrm{hom}}^{\star}
and
D_{\rm cst}
are respectively 2.37, 1.77 and 17.78. This shows that the optimal diffusion coefficients obtained by our numerical procedure or the homogenized diffusion coefficient can be used to faster sample the configurational space, especially in the case of metastable dynamics.

(a)

Mean transition time.

(b)

Metropolis–Hastings rejection probability.

Figure 11
:
Transition times with RWMH for the three optimal diffusion coefficients with respect to the time step
\Delta t
.

7
Perspectives

This work calls for various perspectives and extensions, in particular:

•

A key issue remains to find a relevant normalization constraint
e.g.
based on a numerical criterion, as discussed in
Remark

2
. One way to address this question would be to look at the fully time-discretized problem,
e.g.
optimizing the spectral gap of the transition kernel of the Metropolis Hastings algorithm introduced in Section
6.1
with respect to
\Delta t\mathcal{D}
;

•

In various contexts, we restrict ourselves to isotropic diffusion matrices or even to the one-dimensional setting (in particular for numerical simulations). Considering a larger class of positive definite symmetric matrices may be important to tackle anisotropic targets;

•

In order to apply the methodology to actual systems of interest, one needs to adapt our numerical approach to higher dimensional scenarios, even in the case of a scalar diffusion coefficient,
i.e.
when the diffusion matrix is a multiple of the identity. It is indeed impossible to numerically optimize a diffusion matrix which genuinely depends on the full configuration
q
using discretization techniques such as finite elements, because of the curse of dimensionality of the corresponding approximation space. Apart from resorting to parsimonious functional representations of the diffusion matrix (which usually presuppose some form of smoothness), it seems appropriate to look for diffusion matrices parameterized by a low dimensional function
\xi(q)
, a so-called reaction coordinate in molecular dynamics
[
51
]
, which summarizes some important information on the system. In this context, the potential energy function is replaced by the free energy associated with
\xi
, and the optimization problem can again be reformulated in a low dimensional setting where finite element methods can be used. Work in this direction is in progress
[
53
]
;

•

In this paper we consider the problem of maximizing the spectral gap. The self-adjointness of the generator implies that maximizing the spectral gap is typically equivalent to minimizing the asymptotic variance, uniformly over the space of
L^{2}(\mu)
observables
[
6
]
. It would be interesting to explore the combination of spectral gap maximization and asymptotic variance minimization, as well as minimizing an appropriate cost functional that measures the true cost of the actual computation, by using the approach developed in
[
35
]
;

•

The objective in this paper is to optimize the convergence in
\chi^{2}
-divergence (see (
5
)). A natural question would be to consider convergence in other distances, such as relative entropy with respect to the target distribution (which amounts to optimizing Logarithmic Sobolev Inequality constants).

Appendix A
Proof of
Theorem

4

The fact that the optimization problem is well posed follows by a straightforward adaptation of
[
42
, Theorem 10.3.1]
. Recall that, by
Lemma

3
, the application
\mathcal{D}\mapsto\Lambda(\mathcal{D})
is concave. Moreover, the function
\Lambda
is upper-semicontinuous for the weak-*
L^{\infty}_{V}
topology since it is defined in (
8
)
as the pointwise (in
\mathcal{D}
) infimum of the family of functions

\mathcal{D}\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u(q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)\nabla u(q)\,\mu(q)\,dq\Big/\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(q)^{2}\mu(q)\,dq,

indexed by
u\in H^{1,0}(\mu)\setminus\{0\}
, which are continuous in
\mathcal{D}
for the weak-*
L^{\infty}_{V}
topology.
In particular,
\Lambda
is upper-semicontinuous for the weak-*
L^{\infty}_{V}
topology on
\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
for any
a\geqslant 0
,
b\geqslant 0
and
p\geqslant 1
. Moreover, for any
1\leqslant p\leqslant+\infty
, the subset
\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
is weakly closed for the norm
L^{p}_{V}
defined in (
12
) (this follows directly from the weak closedness of standard Lebesgue spaces). We finally note that the set
\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
is nonempty by
Lemma

1
and compact for the weak-*
L^{\infty}_{V}
topology, for
a\geqslant 0
and
b>0
. This already shows that there exists a solution to (
15
).

To prove that
\mathcal{D}^{\star}
necessarily takes a nonzero value on any open subset
\Omega
of
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, we rely on the following lemma, which shows that the optimal diffusion matrix
\mathcal{D}^{\star}(q)
cannot be degenerate on an open set when
a=0
(for
a>0
, the diffusion is of course nondegenerate).

Lemma 16
.

Fix
a=0
. For any open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}
, there exists
q\in\Omega
such that
\mathcal{D}^{\star}(q)\neq 0
.

Proof.

Let us first prove that
\Lambda(\mathcal{D}^{\star})>0
.
Since the potential function
V\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, it is bounded and the density
\mu
is bounded from below by a positive constant and bounded from above on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. The measure with density (
1
) therefore satisfies a Poincaré inequality,
i.e.
there exists
C_{\mu}>0
such that

\inf_{u\in H^{1,0}(\mu)}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla u(q)|^{2}\,\mu(q)\,dq\Big/\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(q)^{2}\,\mu(q)\,dq\right\}=\frac{1}{C_{\mu}}.

(61)

In addition, there exists
u_{\mu}\in H^{1,0}(\mu)\setminus\{0\}
such that the infimum (
61
) is attained.
Consider

c=\min\left\{\frac{1}{\left|\mathrm{Id}_{d}\right|_{\mathrm{F}}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{e}^{-pV(q)}\,dq\right)^{-1/p},\frac{1}{b}\right\}.

Notice that
\mathcal{D}(q)=c\mathrm{Id}_{d}
is in the constrained set
\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
.
Then,

\Lambda(\mathcal{D}^{\star})\geqslant\Lambda\left(c\mathrm{Id}_{d}\right)=c\inf\limits_{u\in H^{1,0}(\mu)\setminus\left\{0\right\}}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla u(q)|^{2}\mu(q)\,dq\Big/\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(q)^{2}\mu(q)\,dq\right\}=\frac{c}{C_{\mu}}>0.

(62)

To prove the result of Lemma
16
, we now proceed by contradiction.
Assume that there exists an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}
such that
\mathcal{D}^{\star}(q)=0
for all
q\in\Omega
. We can then consider a nonzero function
u_{\Omega}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that
\supp(u_{\Omega})\subset\Omega
, which leads to

0\leqslant\Lambda(\mathcal{D}^{\star})\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u_{\Omega}(q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}^{\star}(q)\nabla u_{\Omega}(q)\,\mu(q)\,dq\Big/\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{\Omega}(q)^{2}\mu(q)\,dq=0.

The latter equality contradicts (
62
) and thus provides the desired result.
∎

Appendix B
Formal characterization of the maximizer using the smooth-min approach

We first construct the counterpart of the map
\mathsf{m}_{\alpha}
in our framework. In order to do so, we fix
\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}_{V}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
such that
\mathcal{D}\geqslant c\mathrm{Id}_{d}
for some
c>0
(see (
21
)). We denote by
g_{\alpha}\colon x\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha x}
and
h_{\alpha}\colon x\mapsto x\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha x}
. By functional calculus, the maps
g_{\alpha}
and
h_{\alpha}
can be generalized to functions of the (positive) self-adjoint operator
-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
. With an abuse of notation, we write in this case
g_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})
and
h_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})
instead of
g_{\alpha}(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})
and
h_{\alpha}(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})
. Since
\mathcal{D}
is bounded and uniformly bounded from below by a positive constant (in the sense of symmetric matrices), the positive operator
-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
has discrete spectrum, its minimum eigenvalue being simple and equal to 0, see
Section

2.1
. We therefore write

-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}=\sum_{i\geqslant 1}\lambda_{i}P_{i},

(63)

where
\lambda_{1}=0<\lambda_{2}<\ldots<\lambda_{i}<\ldots
are the distinct eigenvalues of
-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
and
P_{i}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\Ker(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}-\lambda_{i}\mathrm{Id})
. The multiplicity of
\lambda_{i}
is denoted by
N_{i}=\mathrm{dim}\,\mathrm{Ran}(P_{i})\geqslant 1
. For
\alpha>0
, the map
f_{\alpha}
defined in (
27
) thus rewrites

f_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})=\frac{\Tr(h_{\alpha}(\Diff))}{\Tr(g_{\alpha}(\Diff))-1},

where the operators
g_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D}),h_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})
are defined by functional calculus,
i.e.

g_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})=\sum_{i\geqslant 1}g_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})P_{i},\qquad h_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})=\sum_{i\geqslant 2}h_{\alpha}(\lambda_{i})P_{i},

where we used that
h_{\alpha}(\lambda_{1})=0
. The ratio (
27
) is well-defined in view of
Lemma

19
and the fact that
\Tr\left(g_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})\right)>1
. Formally, since
\lambda_{1}=0
and
N_{1}=1
,

f_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})=\sum_{i\geqslant 2}N_{i}\lambda_{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\lambda_{i}}\Big/{\sum_{i\geqslant 2}}N_{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\lambda_{i}},

so that, again formally (compare with (
26
)),

f_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})=\frac{\lambda_{2}\left(1+\dfrac{1}{N_{2}\lambda_{2}}\displaystyle{\sum_{i\geqslant 3}}N_{i}\lambda_{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{2})}\right)}{1+\dfrac{1}{N_{2}}\displaystyle{\sum_{i\geqslant 3}}N_{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{2})}}\xrightarrow[\alpha\to+\infty]{}\lambda_{2}=\min\limits_{i\geqslant 2}\lambda_{i}.

(64)

This limit is justified after the statement of
Lemma

19
using the dominated convergence theorem. This shows that the quantity
f_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})
is an approximation of
\Lambda(\mathcal{D})
.

This motivates considering the approximation of the problem (
15
) considered in (
28
). One can observe that, for a fixed
\mathcal{D}
,
f_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})
is a convex combination of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
, which implies that
f_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})\geqslant\Lambda(\mathcal{D})
. Therefore, it holds
f_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D}^{\star,\alpha})\geqslant\Lambda(\mathcal{D}^{\star})
, that we indeed observed numerically.

As in
Section

3.2.1
, we assume that there exists a solution to (
28
) for
a=0
and some
b>0
, which satisfies (see (
18
)-(
21
))

\exists b_{+}>b,\quad\mathcal{D}^{\star,\alpha}(q)\,\mathrm{e}^{-V(q)}\leqslant\frac{1}{b_{+}}\,\mathrm{Id}_{d}\textrm{ for a.e. }q\in\mathbb{T}^{d},

(65)

and

\exists c>0,\quad\mathcal{D}^{\star,\alpha}(q)\geqslant c\,\mathrm{Id}_{d}\textrm{ for a.e. }q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}.

(66)

In particular, it holds
\Phi_{p}(\mathcal{D}^{\star,\alpha})=1
. In our numerical experiments, we observe that (
65
) is always satisfied for
b
sufficiently small. Moreover, we use (
66
) below for technical reasons (in order to rigorously define
f_{\alpha}
and prove that it is differentiable). However, we will observe numerically that the characterization that we finally obtain on
\mathcal{D}^{\star,\infty}
(by sending
\alpha\to+\infty
, see Equation (
32
)) seems to also hold even when (
66
) is not satisfied.

Characterization of the optimal diffusion matrix for a finite value of
\alpha
.

The map
f_{\alpha}
defined by (
27
) is smooth over the set of functions
\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}_{V}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
such that
\mathcal{D}\geqslant c\mathrm{Id}_{d}
for some
c>0
, and therefore its differential can be computed even when some eigenvalues are degenerate. The main result of this paragraph is the computation of the differentials of the map
g_{\alpha}
and
h_{\alpha}
, whose proof is given in
Section

B.1
. Note that the derivatives of the maps
g_{\alpha}
and
h_{\alpha}
, namely the maps
g^{\prime}_{\alpha}
and
h^{\prime}_{\alpha}
defined by

\forall x\in\mathbb{R},\quad\forall\alpha>0,\qquad g_{\alpha}^{\prime}(x)=-\alpha\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha x},\qquad h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(x)=(1-\alpha x)\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha x},

can also be generalized to functions of the self-adjoint operator
-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
, and we then write
g_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D})=g_{\alpha}^{\prime}(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})
and
h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D})=h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})
.

Lemma 17
.

Fix
\alpha>0
and
\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}_{V}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
such that
\mathcal{D}\geqslant c\mathrm{Id}_{d}
for some
c>0
. Then there exists
K\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that, for any
\delta\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{S}_{d})
satisfying
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leqslant c/2
, the operators
g_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}),g_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D}),g_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D})\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}
and the operators
h_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}),h_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D}),h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D})\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}
are trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
and

\left\{\begin{aligned} \left\lvert\Tr(g_{\alpha}(\Diff+\delta\Diff))-\Tr(g_{\alpha}(\Diff))-\Tr\left(g_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D})\left(-\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\right)\right)\right\rvert&\leqslant K\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})},\\
\left\lvert\Tr(h_{\alpha}(\Diff+\delta\Diff))-\Tr(h_{\alpha}(\Diff))-\Tr\left(h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D})\left(-\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\right)\right)\right\rvert&\leqslant K\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.\end{aligned}\right.

(67)

This result immediately implies a differentiability result for the map
f_{\alpha}
.

Corollary 18
.

Fix
\alpha>0
and
\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}_{V}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
such that
\mathcal{D}\geqslant c\mathrm{Id}_{d}
for some
c>0
. Then there exists
K\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that, for any
\delta\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{S}_{d})
satisfying
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leqslant c/2
,

\left\lvert f_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D})-f_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})-\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})(\delta\mathcal{D})\right\rvert\leqslant K\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})},

with

\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})(\delta\mathcal{D})=\frac{\Tr\left(h^{\prime}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})\left(-\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\right)\right)\left[\Tr(g_{\alpha}(\Diff))-1\right]-\Tr(h_{\alpha}(\Diff))\Tr\left(g^{\prime}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})\left(-\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\right)\right)}{\left(\Tr(g_{\alpha}(\Diff))-1\right)^{2}}.

Straightforward computations lead to

\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})(\delta\mathcal{D})=\sum\limits_{i\geqslant 1}\left[\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}(1-\alpha\lambda_{i})+\alpha\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}}{\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\lambda_{i}}\right]\Tr\left(P_{i}\left(-\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\right)\right),

with

\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{j\geqslant 2}N_{j}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\lambda_{j}},\qquad\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{j\geqslant 2}N_{j}\lambda_{j}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\lambda_{j}}.

(68)

We now show how to obtain the characterization of
\mathcal{D}^{\star,\alpha}
solution to (
28
) given in (
29
), following the same computations as in the proof of
Proposition

5
. This requires to introduce a Hilbert basis
(e_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
composed of
L^{2}(\mu)
normalized eigenvectors of
-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
, namely

\forall k,\ell\geqslant 1,\qquad-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}e_{k}=\text{\char 21\relax}_{k}e_{k},\qquad\left\langle e_{k},e_{\ell}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}=\delta_{k,\ell},

where
\delta
is the Kronecker symbol and the eigenvalues
\text{\char 21\relax}_{1}=0\leqslant\text{\char 21\relax}_{2}\leqslant\dots\leqslant\text{\char 21\relax}_{k}\leqslant\dots
are ordered and counted with their multiplicities:

\forall i\geqslant 1,\quad\forall k\in\left\{1+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}N_{j},\dots,\sum_{j=1}^{i}N_{j}\right\},\qquad\text{\char 21\relax}_{k}=\lambda_{i}.

We rewrite the spectral decomposition (
63
) of
-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
in terms of this given Hilbert basis and with possibly degenerate eigenvalues: for a given smooth function
\varphi
,

-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\varphi=\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\text{\char 21\relax}_{k}\left\langle\varphi,e_{k}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}e_{k}.

We exploit the Euler–Lagrange equation for the objective function
f_{\alpha}
, namely that
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D}^{\star,\alpha})
is proportional to the differential of the function
\Phi_{p}
(
20
):

\forall\delta\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{S}_{d}),\qquad\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D}^{\star,\alpha})(\delta\mathcal{D})\propto\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left\lvert\mathcal{D}^{\star,\alpha}(q)\right\rvert_{\mathrm{F}}^{p-2}\mathrm{e}^{-pV(q)}\mathcal{D}^{\star,\alpha}(q):\delta\mathcal{D}(q)\,dq.

Since

P_{i}=\sum_{k=1+\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}N_{l}}^{\sum_{l=1}^{i}N_{l}}\left\langle e_{k},\cdot\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}e_{k}

and

\displaystyle\Tr\left(\left\langle e_{k},\cdot\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}e_{k}\nabla^{*}\delta\mathcal{D}\nabla\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}e_{k}(q)\left[\nabla^{*}\delta\mathcal{D}\nabla e_{k}\right](q)\mu(q)\,dq

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla e_{k}(q)^{\top}\delta\mathcal{D}(q)\nabla e_{k}(q)\mu(q)\,dq

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\delta\mathcal{D}(q):\left(\nabla e_{k}(q)\otimes\nabla e_{k}(q)\right)\mu(q)\,dq,

one readily obtains (
29
). Note that, since
\mathcal{D}^{\star,\alpha}
depends on
\alpha
, its eigenvalues also depend on
\alpha
so that the quantities
\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}
first defined in (
68
) are changed accordingly, see (
30
). Note that we used that
\nabla e_{1,\alpha}=0
to start the sum at
k=2
in (
29
) since the eigenspace associated to
\text{\char 21\relax}_{1,\alpha}=0
, which is one-dimensional, is spanned by constant functions.

Formal characterization in the limit
\alpha\to+\infty
and in dimension 1.

We now show how to obtain the formal characterization (
32
).

In the case
\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\infty}<\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\infty}
, the limit when
\alpha\to+\infty
of the sum on the right-hand side of (
31
) can be computed as

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geqslant 2}\left[\frac{\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}(1-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{k,\alpha})+\alpha\mathcal{H}_{\alpha}}{\mathcal{G}_{\alpha}^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{k,\alpha}}\right]|e^{\prime}_{k,\alpha}|^{2}

(69)

\displaystyle\underset{\alpha\to+\infty}{\sim}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\infty}}(1-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\infty})+\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\infty}}}{\mathrm{e}^{-2\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\infty}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\infty}}|e^{\prime}_{2,\alpha}|^{2}=|e^{\prime}_{2,\alpha}|^{2}.

The characterization of the optimal diffusion matrix (
31
) becomes in the limit
\alpha\to+\infty
:

\mathcal{D}^{\star,\infty}(q)=\widetilde{\gamma}_{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{V(q)}\left\lvert e_{2,\infty}^{\prime}(q)\right\rvert^{2/(p-1)},

with
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\infty}>0
. We therefore recover Equation (
24
) of
Section

3.2.1
.

When
\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\infty}=\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\infty}
, the factor in front of
|e^{\prime}_{2,\alpha}|^{2}
in (
69
) is asymptotically equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha}}}{\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha}}+\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\alpha}}\right)^{2}}\left[\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha}}+\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\alpha}}\right)\left(1-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha}\right)+\alpha\left(\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha}}+\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\alpha}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{\left(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha(\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\alpha}-\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha})}\right)^{2}}\left[\left(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha(\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\alpha}-\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha})}\right)(1-\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha})+\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha}+\alpha\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha(\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\alpha}-\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha})}\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{\left(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha(\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\alpha}-\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha})}\right)^{2}}\left[1+\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha(\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\alpha}-\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha})}+\alpha(\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\alpha}-\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha})\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha(\text{\char 21\relax}_{3,\alpha}-\text{\char 21\relax}_{2,\alpha})}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\xrightarrow[\alpha\to+\infty]{}\frac{1+\mathrm{e}^{-\eta}+\eta\mathrm{e}^{-\eta}}{(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\eta})^{2}},

while the factor in front of
|e_{3,\alpha}^{\prime}|^{2}
is asymptotically equivalent to
\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\eta}(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\eta}-\eta)}{(1+\mathrm{e}^{-\eta})^{2}}
. One therefore obtains the characterization given by (
32
).

We check in
Figure

12
that the characterization of the optimal diffusion coefficient using a finite value of
\alpha
,
i.e.
formula (
31
), is valid, using similar approximations as for (
35
): more precisely, for
n\in\left\{1,\dots,N\right\}
, the
n
-th component of the discrete approximation of (
31
) is given by

\widetilde{\gamma}_{\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{V((n-1)/N)}\left(\sum_{k=2}^{N}\left[\frac{G_{\alpha}(D^{\star,\alpha})(1-\alpha\sigma_{k}(D^{\star,\alpha}))+\alpha H_{\alpha}(D^{\star,\alpha})}{G_{\alpha}(D^{\star,\alpha})^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\sigma_{k}(D^{\star,\alpha})}\right]\left\lvert U_{k,\alpha,n}-U_{k,\alpha,n-1}\right\rvert^{2}\right)^{1/(p-1)},

where
G_{\alpha},H_{\alpha}
are defined in (
104
),
(U_{k,\alpha})_{2\leqslant k\leqslant N}
are the normalized eigenvectors associated with the eigenvalues
(\sigma_{k}(D^{\star,\alpha}))_{2\leqslant k\leqslant N}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\alpha}
is a normalizing constant. Note that this formula requires all the eigenelements which can be computationally heavy.

Figure 12
:
Comparison between
D^{\star,\alpha}
with
\alpha=1.0
and
D^{\star}
.

B.1
Proof of
Lemma

17

Lemma

17
relies on technical results which are proved in
Section

B.2
. Let us introduce some notation and recall standard results for trace-class operators that will be useful below. For two Banach spaces
E,F
, we denote by
\mathcal{B}(E,F)
the Banach space of bounded linear operators from
E
to
F
. We denote by
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{1}=\Tr(A^{*}A)^{1/2}
the trace norm of an operator
A
on
L^{2}(\mu)
. Note that if, for any
s\in[0,1]
, the operator
A(s)
is a trace-class operator on
L^{2}(\mu)
and there exist
K\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\left\lVert A(s)\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant K
for any
s\in[0,1]
, then

\left\lVert\int_{0}^{1}A(s)ds\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}\left\lVert A(s)\right\rVert_{1}ds\leqslant K.

(70)

We also recall that if
A
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
and
B
is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
, the operators
AB
and
BA
are trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
and

\left\lVert AB\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mu))}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{1},\qquad\left\lVert BA\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mu))}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{1}

(71)

We refer to
[
63
,
74
]
for more detailed results on trace-class operators.

To prove (
67
), we first assume that
\delta\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{S}_{d})
with
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leqslant c/2
, and show the inequality (
67
) with a bound
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}
on the right-hand side. The desired result for any
\delta\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{S}_{d})
such that
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leqslant c/2
is then obtained by a density argument.

We perform the computations for the map
g_{\alpha}
, a similar reasoning leading to the result for the map
h_{\alpha}
. In this proof, the letter
K
denotes a generic nonnegative finite constant which may change from line to line.

Consider
\delta\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{S}_{d})
such that
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leqslant c/2
. Then
\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}\geqslant(c/2)\mathrm{Id}_{d}
so that the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}
is negative and self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\mu)
. We can therefore use Duhamel’s formula twice to obtain

\displaystyle g_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D})-g_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}-\mathrm{e}^{\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}=\left[\mathrm{e}^{s\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\right]_{s=0}^{s=1}

\displaystyle=\alpha\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{e}^{s\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}ds

\displaystyle=\alpha\int_{0}^{1}\left(\mathrm{e}^{s\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}+\left[\mathrm{e}^{s(1-u)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathrm{e}^{su\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}\right]_{u=0}^{u=1}\right)\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}ds

\displaystyle=\alpha\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{e}^{s\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}ds+\alpha^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{e}^{s(1-u)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{su\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}s\,du\,ds

\displaystyle=\alpha\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{e}^{s\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}ds+\alpha^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{e}^{(s-u)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{u\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}du\,ds,

(72)

where we performed the change of variables
u\leftarrow su
for the last equality.

By
Lemma

19
below, the operators
g_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D})
and
g_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})
are trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
. The operator defined by the first term on the right-hand side of (
72
) is linear in
\delta\mathcal{D}
. We now show that it is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
and that there exists
K\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\left\lVert\alpha\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{e}^{s\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}ds\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant K\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

(73)

For any
s\in[0,1]
, let
T_{s}=\mathrm{e}^{s\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
. We need to distinguish between the two cases
0\leqslant s\leqslant 1/2
and
1/2\leqslant s\leqslant 1
: when
0\leqslant s\leqslant 1/2
, the operator
\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
, possibly multiplied by any polynomial of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
, is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
uniformly in
s
thanks to
Lemma

19
, while the operator
\mathrm{e}^{s\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
, so that (
71
) can be used. A similar result is obtained in the case
1/2\leqslant s\leqslant 1
by switching
\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
and
\mathrm{e}^{s\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
in the reasoning.

More precisely, for
s\in[0,1/2]
, we rewrite
T_{s}
as

T_{s}=\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{s\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}}_{=:A_{1}}\underbrace{\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{-1}}_{=:A_{2}}\underbrace{(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}}_{=:A_{3}}.

Note that
\left\lVert A_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mu))}\leqslant 1
, and
A_{2}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
by
Lemma

22
(see (
80
) below). The operator
A_{3}
is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
by
Lemma

19
, so that
T_{s}
is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
with trace norm uniformly bounded over
s\in\left[0,1/2\right]
. In view of (
71
) and (
80
), there exists
K\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\forall s\in[0,1/2],\qquad\left\lVert T_{s}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant K\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

In the case
1/2\leqslant s\leqslant 1
, we rewrite
T_{s}
as

T_{s}=\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{s\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})}_{:=A_{3}}\underbrace{(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}}_{=:A_{2}}\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}}_{=:A_{1}},

and proceed with a similar argument as above. In view of (
70
), the operator defined by the first term on the right-hand side of (
72
) is therefore trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
and (
73
) holds.

We now show that the operator defined by the second term on the right-hand side of (
72
) is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
, and that its trace norm is bounded from above by
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}
. First, note that the operator
