(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{2}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
for any
\theta>0
. Indeed, let us rewrite this operator as
(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{D+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{2}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{-2}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{2}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
, and let us set
T=(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{D+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{2}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{-2}
.
Lemma

21
then implies that

0\leqslant T^{*}T\leqslant(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{-2}\left(\mathrm{Id}-\frac{\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}+\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}}{c}\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\right)^{4}\left(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\right)^{-2}.

(74)

Recall that if
A\leqslant B
as symmetric operators on a Hilbert space, then
C^{*}AC\leqslant C^{*}BC
for any bounded operator
C
on the same Hilbert space. The operator on the right-hand side of (
74
) is therefore bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
by functional calculus, so that
T
is also bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
.
Lemma

19
then implies that the operators
(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{2}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
are trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
, and more precisely that for any
\underline{\theta}>0
, there exists
K\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
(which depends on
\underline{\theta}
) such that

\forall\theta>\underline{\theta},\qquad\left\lVert(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{2}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant K.

(75)

For any
s\in[0,1]
and
u\in[0,s]
, let
T_{s,u}=\mathrm{e}^{\alpha(s-u)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{u\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
. We need to distinguish between three cases: if
0\leqslant s\leqslant 1/2
, the operator
\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
uniformly in
s
and the other operators are bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
; if
1/2\leqslant s\leqslant 1
, then the operator
\mathrm{e}^{\alpha(s-u)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
uniformly in
u,s
for
0\leqslant u\leqslant s/2
, while it is the operator
\mathrm{e}^{\alpha u\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}
which is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
uniformly in
u,s
for
s/2\leqslant u\leqslant s
.

If
s\in[0,1/2]
, we rewrite
T_{s,u}
as

T_{s,u}=\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{(s-u)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}}_{=:A_{1}}\underbrace{\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{-1}}_{=:A_{2}}\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{u\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}}_{=:A_{3}}\underbrace{(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{-2}}_{=:A_{4}}\underbrace{(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{2}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}}_{=:A_{5}}.

It holds
\left\lVert A_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mu))}\leqslant 1
and
\left\lVert A_{3}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mu))}\leqslant 1
. The operators
A_{2}
and
A_{4}
are bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
by
Lemma

22
, while the operator
A_{5}
is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
by (
75
). Moreover, in view of (
71
), (
75
), (
80
) and (
82
), there exists
K\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that, for any
s\in[0,1/2]
and any
u\in[0,s]
,

\left\lVert T_{s,u}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant K\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

(76)

If
s\in[1/2,1]
and
u\in[0,s/2]
, we rewrite
T_{s,u}
as

T_{s,u}=\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{(s-u)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{2}}_{=:A_{5}}\underbrace{(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{-2}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})}_{=:A_{4}}\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{u\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}}_{=:A_{3}}\underbrace{(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}}_{=:A_{2}}\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}}_{=:A_{1}},

and if
s\in[1/2,1]
and
u\in[s/2,s]
, we rewrite
T_{s,u}
as

T_{s,u}=\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{(s-u)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}}_{=:A_{1}}\underbrace{\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{-1}}_{=:A_{2}}\underbrace{(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})\mathrm{e}^{u\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})}_{=:A_{5}}\underbrace{(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}}_{=:A_{4}}\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}}_{=:A_{3}}.

We then perform manipulations similar to the ones leading to (
76
) in the case
0\leqslant s\leqslant 1/2
.

In view of (
70
), the operator in (
72
) is therefore trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
, and there exists
K\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\left\lVert\alpha^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{e}^{\alpha(s-u)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{u\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}du\,ds\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant K\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}.

We can at this stage take the trace in the equality (
72
). In particular, using the linearity and the cyclicity of the trace, the trace of the operator defined by the first term on the right-hand side of (
72
) is

\displaystyle\Tr\left(\alpha\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{e}^{s\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}ds\right)

\displaystyle=\alpha\int_{0}^{1}\Tr(
\rme^{s\alpha\cLD}\cL_{\delta\Diff}\rme^{(1-s)\alpha\cLD}
)ds

\displaystyle=\alpha\int_{0}^{1}\Tr(
\rme^{\alpha\cLD}\cL_{\delta\Diff}
)ds

\displaystyle=\alpha\Tr(\rme^{\alpha\cLD}\cL_{\delta\Diff})=\Tr(g_{\alpha}'(\Diff)\left(-\cL_{\delta\Diff}\right)).

This shows that (
67
) hold with the bound
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}
on the right-hand side.

Using the density of
\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{S}_{d})
in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{S}_{d})
, we now prove that (
67
) holds. It suffices to show that the trace of the operator defined by the second term on the right-hand side of (
72
) is bounded from above by
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}
. We compute, using Fubini’s theorem,

\displaystyle\Tr\left(\alpha^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{s}\mathrm{e}^{\alpha(s-u)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{u\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}du\,ds\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\alpha^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{s}\Tr\left(\mathrm{e}^{(s-u)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{u\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-s)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\right)du\,ds

\displaystyle\qquad=\alpha^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{s}\Tr\left(\mathrm{e}^{(1-u)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{u\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\right)du\,ds

\displaystyle\qquad=\alpha^{2}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{u}^{1}\Tr\left(\mathrm{e}^{u\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-u)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\right)ds\,du

\displaystyle\qquad=\alpha^{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-u)\Tr\left(\mathrm{e}^{u\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-u)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\right)du.

Denote by
V_{u}=\mathrm{e}^{u\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{e}^{(1-u)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}
. If
u\in[0,1/2]
, we rewrite
V_{u}
as

V_{u}=\underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{u\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}}}_{=:A_{1}}\underbrace{\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{-1}}_{=:A_{2}}\underbrace{(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})\mathrm{e}^{(1-u)\alpha\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})}_{=:A_{3}}\underbrace{(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}}_{=:A_{4}}.

It holds
\left\lVert A_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mu))}\leqslant 1
, the operators
A_{2}
and
A_{4}
are bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
by
Lemma

22
, while the operator
A_{3}
is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
by
Lemma

19
. In view of (
71
) and (
80
), there exists
K\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that, for all
u\in[0,1/2]
,

\left\lVert V_{u}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant K\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

If
u\in[1/2,1]
, using the cyclicity of the trace, we write

\Tr(V_u)=\Tr(
\underbrace{(\Id-\cLD)\rme^{u\alpha\cL_{\Diff+\delta\Diff}}(\Id-\cLD)}_{=:A_1}
\underbrace{(\Id-\cLD)^{-1}\cL_{\delta\Diff}}_{=:A_2}
\underbrace{\rme^{(1-u)\alpha\cLD}}_{=:A_3}
\underbrace{\cL_{\delta\Diff}(\Id-\cLD)^{-1}}_{=:A_4}
),

and proceed as above.
The inequality

\left\lvert\int_{0}^{1}(1-u)\Tr\left(V_{u}\right)\,du\right\rvert\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}\left\lvert\Tr\left(V_{u}\right)\right\rvert\,du\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}\left\lVert V_{u}\right\rVert_{1}\,du,

then implies (
67
), which concludes the proof.

B.2
Technical results

The following lemma (whose proof is given in
Section

B.2.1
) shows that
f_{\alpha}
is well-defined whenever
\alpha>0
and
\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}_{V}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
satisfies
\mathcal{D}\geqslant c\mathrm{Id}_{d}
for some
c>0
.

Lemma 19
.

Fix
\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}_{V}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
such that
\mathcal{D}\geqslant c\mathrm{Id}_{d}
for some
c>0
. Then, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\theta>0
, the operators
\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\right)^{n}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
and
(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{n}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
are trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
. Moreover, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\underline{\theta}>0
, there exists
\theta^{\star}>0
and
K\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
(which both depend on
n
and
\underline{\theta}
) such that

\forall\theta\geqslant\underline{\theta},\qquad\left\lVert\left(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\right)^{n}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\right\rVert_{1}\leqslant K.

(77)

Using the notation of
Section

3.2.2
, the limit in (
64
) is therefore obtained with the dominated convergence theorem, using that
\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{2})}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{2})}
for any
\alpha\geqslant 1
and that the sums
\sum_{i\geqslant 3}N_{i}\lambda_{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{i}}
and
\sum_{i\geqslant 3}N_{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda_{i}}
are finite since the operators
g_{1}(\mathcal{D})
and
h_{1}(\mathcal{D})
are trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
.

The three following lemmas are useful to bound various terms when identifying the differentials of
\mathcal{D}\mapsto g_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})
and
\mathcal{D}\mapsto h_{\alpha}(\mathcal{D})
. More precisely, the following lemma allows to compare the operators
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}
as a function of
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
.

Lemma 20
.

Fix
\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}_{V}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
such that
\mathcal{D}\geqslant c\mathrm{Id}_{d}
for some
c>0
. Then for any
\delta\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{S}_{d})
,

\frac{1}{c}\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\leqslant\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\leqslant-\frac{1}{c}\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}.

(78)

The result is an immediate consequence of the inequality

\delta\mathcal{D}\leqslant\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\mathrm{Id}_{d}\leqslant\frac{1}{c}\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\mathcal{D}.

Likewise, the following lemma (proved in
Section

B.2.2
) allows to compare the operators
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}
for a small enough perturbation
\delta\mathcal{D}
.

Lemma 21
.

Fix
\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}_{V}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
such that
\mathcal{D}\geqslant c\mathrm{Id}_{d}
for some
c>0
, and consider
\delta\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{S}_{d})
such that
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leqslant c/2
. Then,

\frac{\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}+\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}}{c}\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\leqslant\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}\leqslant\frac{c}{2\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\leqslant 0.

(79)

Lastly, the following result (proved in
Section

B.2.3
) shows that some operators appearing in the proof of
Lemma

17
are bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
, with an explicit bound on
\delta\mathcal{D}
when needed. Note that the bound (
82
) is not written in terms of the
L^{\infty}
norm of
\delta\mathcal{D}
, but rather as a function of the
\mathcal{C}^{3}
norm of
\delta\mathcal{D}
. This may be a technical limitation due to our technique of proof, which involves commutators, but the estimate is sufficient for our purposes.

Lemma 22
.

Fix
\mathcal{D}\in L^{\infty}_{V}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
such that
\mathcal{D}\geqslant c\mathrm{Id}_{d}
for some
c>0
, and consider
\delta\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{S}_{d})
such that
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leqslant c/2
. Then the following statements hold:

•

The operators
\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{-1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{-1}
are bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
. More precisely, there exists
K\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\left\lVert\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mu))}+\left\lVert\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mu))}\leqslant K\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})};

(80)

•

The operator
(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{2}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
for any
\theta>0
. More precisely, for any
\underline{\theta}>0
, there exists
K_{\underline{\theta}}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that, for all
\theta>\underline{\theta}
,

\left\lVert(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{2}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mu))}\leqslant K_{\underline{\theta}};

(81)

•

The operator
(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{-2}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
. More precisely, there exists
K\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\left\lVert(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{-2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mu))}\leqslant K\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})},

(82)

where
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=\sup\limits_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d}\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}_{i,j}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
.

B.2.1
Proof of
Lemma

19

We first show that the operator
\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
for
\theta>0
. Using the lower bound on
\mathcal{D}
, it holds
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\leqslant c\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}}
on
L^{2}(\mu)
. It therefore suffices to show that the operator
\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}}}
is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
. Define the unitary transformation

U\colon\left\{\begin{aligned} L^{2}(\mu)&\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\\
f&\mapsto f\mathrm{e}^{-V/2}.\end{aligned}\right.

(83)

Straightforward computations show that

U\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}}U^{\star}=\Delta+W\leqslant 0

(84)

on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, where
\Delta
is the Laplacian operator with periodic boundary conditions on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, and the smooth function

W:=\frac{1}{2}\Delta V-\frac{1}{4}\left\lvert\nabla V\right\rvert^{2}

(85)

is identified with a multiplication operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. This implies that

0\leqslant\Tr\left(\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\right)\leqslant\Tr\left(\mathrm{e}^{c\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}}}\right)=\Tr_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{c\theta(\Delta+W)}\right)\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{c\theta\mathcal{W}}\Tr_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{c\theta\Delta}\right),

where
\mathcal{W}=\max\limits_{q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left\lvert W(q)\right\rvert
. Since the operator
\mathrm{e}^{c\theta\Delta}
is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for any
\theta>0
(see for instance
[
64
, Theorem XIII.76]
), the operator
\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
for any
\theta>0
.

To conclude the proof, we rely on the fact that, for
\underline{\theta}>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
fixed, there exists
\theta^{\star}>0
and
K>0
such that
(-x)^{n}\mathrm{e}^{\theta x}\leqslant K\mathrm{e}^{\theta^{\star}x}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}_{-}
and any
\theta\geqslant\underline{\theta}
. This implies that the operator
\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\right)^{n}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
is trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. It follows that the operator
(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{n}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
is also trace-class on
L^{2}(\mu)
as it is a linear combination of the operators
\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\right)^{k}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
for
0\leqslant k\leqslant n
. This also implies that the estimate (
77
) holds, and concludes the proof.

B.2.2
Proof of
Lemma

21

Since
\frac{c}{2}\mathrm{Id}_{d}\leqslant\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}\leqslant\left(\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}+\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\right)\mathrm{Id}_{d}
, it holds

\left(\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}+\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\right)\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}}\leqslant\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}}\leqslant\frac{c}{2}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}}\leqslant 0.

(86)

Using now that
c\mathrm{Id}_{d}\leqslant\mathcal{D}\leqslant\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\mathrm{Id}_{d}
, it holds
\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{-1}\mathcal{D}\leqslant\mathrm{Id}_{d}\leqslant c^{-1}\mathcal{D}
so that

c^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\leqslant\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}}\leqslant\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}.

(87)

Combining (
86
) with (
87
) implies (
79
), which concludes the proof.

B.2.3
Proof of
Lemma

22

We prove the first item. Denote by
T=\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{-1}
. Using
Lemma

20
, it holds

0\leqslant T^{*}T\leqslant\frac{1}{c^{2}}\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}^{2}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{-1}.

The operator on the right-hand side of the inequality is bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
since

\left\lVert(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}^{2}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}})^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mu))}\leqslant\sup_{\lambda\geqslant 0}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(1+\lambda)^{2}}=1.

Therefore, the operator
T
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
. Now let
S=\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{-1}
. Using
Lemma

21
, it holds

0\leqslant SS^{*}\leqslant\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathrm{Id}-\frac{c}{2\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\right)^{-2}\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}.

The operator
\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathrm{Id}-\frac{c}{2\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}}\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\right)^{-1}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
using a similar argument as above, with an upper bound proportional to
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
. This implies that the operator
S
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
. The estimate (
80
) easily follows.

We next prove the second item. Denote now by
T_{\theta}=(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{2}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
. In view of
Lemma

21
,

0\leqslant T_{\theta}^{*}T_{\theta}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}\left(\mathrm{Id}-c^{-1}\left(\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}+\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\right)\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\right)^{4}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}.

The operators
\left(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\right)^{i}\mathrm{e}^{\theta\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}}
for
0\leqslant i\leqslant 2
are bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
by functional calculus. This implies that the operator
T_{\theta}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
, and the uniform estimate (
81
) is obtained in the same manner as in the end of the proof of
Lemma

19
.

We finally prove the third item. Let
T=(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{-2}
. Consider
\kappa>0
(a parameter which will be made precise below) and rewrite the operator
T
as

T=\underbrace{(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})^{-1}}_{:=A_{1}}\underbrace{(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})^{-2}}_{:=A_{2}}\underbrace{(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})^{2}(\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}+\delta\mathcal{D}})^{-2}}_{:=A_{3}}.

(88)

The operators
A_{1}
and
A_{3}
are bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
. We prove this statement for the operator
A_{1}
, the proof for
A_{3}
being similar. Using (
86
), it holds

0\leqslant A_{1}^{*}A_{1}\leqslant(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})^{-1}\left(\mathrm{Id}-\left(\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}+\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\right)\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}}\right)^{2}(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})^{-1},

and the operator on the right-hand side of the previous inequality is bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
since

\displaystyle\left\lVert(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})^{-2}\left(\mathrm{Id}-\left(\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}+\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\right)\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}}\right)^{2}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mu))}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\sup\limits_{\lambda\geqslant 0}\frac{1+\left(\left\lVert\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}+\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\right)\lambda}{\kappa+\lambda}\right)^{2}<+\infty.

In view of (
88
), it therefore suffices to show that the operator
(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})\nabla^{*}\delta\mathcal{D}\nabla(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})^{-2}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mu)
, or, equivalently, that the operator

U(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})\nabla^{*}\delta\mathcal{D}\nabla(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})^{-2}U^{*}=U(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})U^{*}\left(U\nabla^{*}\delta\mathcal{D}\nabla U^{*}\right)U(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})^{-2}U^{*},

(89)

is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, where
U
denotes the unitary transformation defined by (
83
). Using (
84
), it holds

U(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})U^{*}=\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\Delta-W,\qquad U(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{Id}_{d}})^{-2}U^{*}=(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\Delta-W)^{-2}.

where
W
is defined in (
85
). Straightforward computations lead to

U\nabla^{*}=\left(-\nabla+\frac{1}{2}\nabla V\right)U,\qquad\nabla U^{*}=U^{*}\left(\nabla+\frac{1}{2}\nabla V\right).

Therefore, the operator in (
89
) can be rewritten as

\left(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\Delta-W\right)\left[\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d}\left(-\partial_{q_{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q_{j}}V\right)\delta\mathcal{D}_{i,j}\left(\partial_{q_{i}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q_{i}}V\right)\right]\left(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\Delta-W\right)^{-2}.

We now choose
\kappa
such that
\kappa-W\geqslant 1
(which is possible since
W
is smooth on the compact space
\mathbb{T}^{d}
), so that
\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\Delta-W\geqslant\mathrm{Id}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. The operators
(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\Delta-W)(\mathrm{Id}-\Delta)^{-1}
and
(\mathrm{Id}-\Delta)^{2}(\kappa\mathrm{Id}-\Delta-W)^{-2}
are then bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, so that it suffices to show that the operator

\left(\mathrm{Id}-\Delta\right)\left[\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d}\left(-\partial_{q_{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q_{j}}V\right)\delta\mathcal{D}_{i,j}\left(\partial_{q_{i}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q_{i}}V\right)\right]\left(\mathrm{Id}-\Delta\right)^{-2}

is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to conclude.

For
k\geqslant 1
, denote by
H^{k}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
the Sobolev space of square integrable functions on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
with all (distributional) derivatives up to order
k
belonging to
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Using the Fourier series characterization of Sobolev spaces and the fact that
V
is smooth, it holds

\left\{\begin{aligned} &(\mathrm{Id}-\Delta)^{-2}\in\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{d})),\\
\forall 1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d,\quad&\partial_{q_{i}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q_{i}}V\in\mathcal{B}(H^{4}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})),\\
\forall 1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d,\quad&-\partial_{q_{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q_{j}}V\in\mathcal{B}(H^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})),\\
&\mathrm{Id}-\Delta\in\mathcal{B}(H^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})).\end{aligned}\right.

To obtain (
82
), it therefore suffices that
\delta\mathcal{D}_{i,j}
, seen as a multiplication operator on
H^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, is bounded uniformly in
i,j\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
with
\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}_{i,j}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}(H^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))}\leqslant K_{i,j}\left\lVert\delta\mathcal{D}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
for a constant
K_{i,j}\geqslant 0
. This is easily seen to hold using the Leibniz rule since
\delta\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{S}_{d})
. Thus, the estimate (
82
) holds, which concludes the proof of
Lemma

22
.

Appendix C
Discretizations of the optimization problems (
15
) and (
28
)

To solve the optimization problems (
15
) and (
28
) in practice, we introduce two finite-dimensional parametrizations: one for the diffusion matrix
\mathcal{D}
, and one for the eigenfunction
u
. We consider for simplicity the case of isotropic diffusion matrices

\mathcal{D}(q)=\mathscr{D}(q)\mathrm{Id}_{d}.

(90)

We introduce a piecewise constant parametrization for
\mathscr{D}
in
Section

C.1
, and next a
\mathbb{P}_{1}
finite element parametrization (
i.e.
using a basis of continuous and piecewise affine functions) for the eigenfunctions
u
in
Section

C.2
, in the one dimensional case
d=1
for simplicity of exposition. The optimization problem then boils down to a constrained generalized eigenvalue problem, see
Section

C.3
. We describe in
Section

C.4
how to solve the latter problem.

C.1
Parametrization of the diffusion matrix

In practice, we parametrize the scalar valued function
\mathscr{D}
in (
90
) using a finite-dimensional vector subspace of
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R})
. More precisely, we introduce a set of
N
non-negative functions
\{\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{N}\}\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathbb{R})
, and consider functions of the generic form

\mathscr{D}_{D}(q)=\sum_{n=1}^{N}D_{n}\psi_{n}(q),\qquad D=(D_{1},\ldots,D_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}.

(91)

Nonnegativity conditions for
\mathscr{D}_{D}
may be cumbersome to write when the functions
\psi_{n}
have supports which overlap in a nontrivial way, as is the case for instance for (tensor products of) trigonometric functions. This motivates us to choose

\psi_{n}(q)=\mathbf{1}_{K_{n}}(q),

(92)

the indicator function of a domain
K_{n}\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}
, with the condition that
(K_{1},\ldots,K_{N})
forms a partition of
\mathbb{T}^{d}
(typically, the sets
K_{n}
are rectangles obtained from a Cartesian mesh). In order for the function (
91
) to be in the set (
16
) for the choice (
92
), the components
(D_{n})_{1\leqslant n\leqslant N}
should belong to the set

\delta_{p}^{a,b}=\left\{D\in\left[a\mathrm{e}^{V_{+}(K_{1})},\frac{1}{b}\mathrm{e}^{V_{-}(K_{1})}\right]\times\dots\times\left[a\mathrm{e}^{V_{+}(K_{N})},\frac{1}{b}\mathrm{e}^{V_{-}(K_{N})}\right]\,\middle|\,\sum_{n=1}^{N}\omega_{p,n}D_{n}^{p}\leqslant 1\right\},

(93)

where

V_{-}(E)=\inf_{E}V,\qquad V_{+}(E)=\sup_{E}V,\qquad\omega_{p,n}=\int_{K_{n}}\mathrm{e}^{-pV}\left\lvert\mathrm{Id}_{d}\right\rvert_{\mathrm{F}}^{p}>0.

We assume in the sequel that the conditions

ab\leqslant\min_{1\leqslant n\leqslant N}\mathrm{e}^{[V_{-}(K_{n})-V_{+}(K_{n})]},\qquad a\leqslant\left(\sum_{n=1}^{N}\omega_{p,n}\mathrm{e}^{pV_{+}(K_{n})}\right)^{-1/p},

(94)

hold, so that the set
\delta_{p}^{a,b}
is not empty. These conditions can be ensured by choosing
a,b\geqslant 0
sufficiently small.

The maximization problem (
15
) can then be approximated by the finite dimensional maximization problem

\textrm{Find }D^{\star}\in\delta_{p}^{a,b}\textrm{ such that }\Lambda(\mathscr{D}_{D^{\star}}\mathrm{Id}_{d})=\sup_{D\in\delta_{p}^{a,b}}\Lambda(\mathscr{D}_{D}\mathrm{Id}_{d}).

(95)

The well-posedness of this optimization problem is guaranteed by the following result.

Proposition 23
.

Fix
N\geqslant 1
and
p\in[1,+\infty)
, and consider a partition
(K_{1},\dots,K_{N})
of
\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
a,b\geqslant 0
such that (
94
) holds. There exists a solution
D^{\star}
to (
95
).

Note that the value
b=0
is allowed for
N
finite, in contrast to
Theorem

4
, since the inequality in (
93
) ensures that all the components
(D_{n})_{1\leqslant n\leqslant N}
are bounded from above by
(\min_{1\leqslant n\leqslant N}\omega_{p,n})^{-1/p}
.

Proof.

The function
D\mapsto\Lambda(\mathscr{D}_{D}\mathrm{Id}_{d})
is upper-semicontinuous on
\delta_{p}^{a,b}
by arguments similar to the ones used in
Appendix

A
(and whatever the choice of the norm on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
). In addition,
\delta_{p}^{a,b}
is closed and bounded, hence compact, which, combined with the upper-semicontinuity of the function to maximize, guarantees the existence of a maximum and thus proves the result.
∎

Likewise, the maximization problem (
28
) can be approximated by the finite dimensional maximization problem

\textrm{Find }D^{\star,\alpha}\in\delta_{p}^{a,b}\textrm{ such that }f_{\alpha}(\mathscr{D}_{D^{\star,\alpha}}\mathrm{Id}_{d})=\sup_{D\in\delta_{p}^{a,b}}f_{\alpha}(\mathscr{D}_{D}\mathrm{Id}_{d}).

The well-posedness of this optimization problem can be obtained by similar arguments.

Notice that one can prove that any solution
D^{\star}
of (
95
) is positive when the domains
K_{n}
have nonempty interiors.

Proposition 24
.

Fix
N\geqslant 1
and
p\in[1,+\infty)
, and consider a partition
(K_{1},\dots,K_{N})
of
\mathbb{T}^{d}
for which the domains
(K_{n})_{1\leqslant n\leqslant N}
have nonempty interiors. Assume that
a,b\geqslant 0
satisfy (
94
), and denote by
{D}^{\star}=(D^{\star}_{1},\ldots,{D}^{\star}_{N})\in\delta_{p}^{a,b}
a solution to the maximization problem (
95
). Then,

\forall n\in\{1,\ldots,N\},\qquad{D}^{\star}_{n}>0.

Note however that this result does not give any information on the behavior of the lower bound on the components of
D^{\star}
in the limit of a vanishing mesh size. The numerical results we present (see for instance
Figure

4
) indicate that the lower bound can indeed converge to 0 as
N\to+\infty
.

Proof.

There exists
\gamma>0
such that
\gamma\mathbbm{1}_{N}\in\delta_{p}^{a,b}
(where
\mathbbm{1}_{N}
is the
N
-dimensional vector whose components are all equal to 1), and thus, since
\mathscr{D}_{\mathbbm{1}_{N}}(q)=1
for any
q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, and in view of the Poincaré inequality (
61
),

\Lambda(\mathscr{D}_{D^{\star}}\mathrm{Id}_{d})\geqslant\gamma\Lambda(\mathscr{D}_{\mathbbm{1}_{N}}\mathrm{Id}_{d})\geqslant\frac{\gamma}{C_{\mu}}.

Consider
n\in\argmin_{m\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}D^{\star}_{p,m}
. Since
K_{n}
has a nonempty interior, there exists an open ball
\mathscr{B}_{n}
such that
\overline{\mathscr{B}_{n}}\subset K_{n}
. We consider the function
v_{n}
defined on
\mathscr{B}_{n}
as a normalized eigenfunction associated with the first eigenvalue of the Laplace problem with Dirichlet boundary conditions:

-\Delta v_{n}=\lambda_{1,n}v_{n}\ \mathrm{on}\ \mathscr{B}_{n},\qquad v_{n}=0\ \mathrm{on}\ \partial\mathscr{B}_{n},\qquad\int_{\mathscr{B}_{n}}v_{n}^{2}=1.

(96)

Note that
\lambda_{1,n}>0
. The function
v_{n}
is then extended by 0 on
\mathbb{T}^{d}\setminus\mathscr{B}_{n}
. It is easy to check that
v_{n}\in H^{1}(\mu)
. We finally define
u_{n}\in H^{1,0}(\mu)
as

u_{n}=v_{n}-\int_{\mathscr{B}_{n}}v_{n}\,\mu.

In view of (
96
), and denoting by
\mu_{+}=\max\limits_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mu<+\infty
,

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathscr{D}_{D^{\star}}|\nabla u_{n}|^{2}\mu=D^{\star}_{p,n}\int_{\mathscr{B}_{n}}|\nabla u_{n}|^{2}\mu\leqslant\mu_{+}D^{\star}_{p,n}\int_{\mathscr{B}_{n}}|\nabla v_{n}|^{2}=\mu_{+}\lambda_{1,n}D^{\star}_{p,n}.

Therefore, since
\|u_{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}>0
(otherwise
v_{n}
would be constant on
\mathscr{B}_{n}
, which is impossible by (
96
)),

\displaystyle\Lambda(\mathscr{D}_{D^{\star}}\mathrm{Id}_{d})

\displaystyle=\inf_{u\in H^{1,0}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathscr{D}_{D^{\star}}(q)|\nabla u(q)|^{2}\mu(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(q)^{2}\mu(q)\,dq}\leqslant\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathscr{D}_{D^{\star}}(q)|\nabla u_{n}(q)|^{2}\mu(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{n}(q)^{2}\mu(q)\,dq}\leqslant\frac{\mu_{+}\lambda_{1,n}D^{\star}_{p,n}}{\|u_{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}}.

This allows to conclude that
D^{\star}_{n}\geqslant\gamma\|u_{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}/(\mu_{+}\lambda_{1,n}C_{\mu})>0
, which leads to the claimed result.
∎

C.2
Finite element approximation of the eigenvalue problem

We discuss in this section how to numerically approximate
\Lambda(\mathcal{D})
for a given diffusion matrix
\mathcal{D}
. For simplicity of exposition, we restrict the presentation to the one dimensional case
d=1
, the extension to higher dimensional situations posing no difficulties in principle (but numerically viable only for
d\leqslant 3
in terms of computational costs).

The minimization in (
8
) is performed over a linear space of dimension
I
obtained by a
\mathbb{P}_{1}
finite element discretization over a regular mesh with nodes

q_{i}=i/I
for
i\in\{0,\ldots,I-1\}
with
q_{-1}\equiv q_{I-1}
,
q_{0}\equiv q_{I}
and
q_{1}\equiv q_{I+1}
to comply with periodic boundary conditions. The continuous basis functions
(\varphi_{i})_{i=1,\dots,I}
have support on
[q_{i-1},q_{i+1}]
and are piecewise affine:

\varphi_{i}(q)=\left\{\begin{aligned} I(q-q_{i-1})&\quad\text{if}\ q\in[q_{i-1},q_{i}],\\
I(q_{i+1}-q)&\quad\text{if}\ q\in[q_{i},q_{i+1}].\end{aligned}\right.

We denote by
S_{I}={\rm Span}(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{I})
, and by

S_{I,0}=\left\{u\in S_{I}\,\middle|\,\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(q)\mu(q)\,dq=0\right\}.

We can then introduce the following mapping, which corresponds to a finite element approximation of
\Lambda(\mathscr{D}_{D})
:

\lambda_{N,I}(D)=\inf_{u\in S_{I,0}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathscr{D}_{D}(q)u^{\prime}(q)^{2}\mu(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(q)^{2}\mu(q)\,dq}.

(97)

Let us recall the standard approach to solve (
97
). Upon writing
u=\sum_{i=1}^{I}U_{i}\varphi_{i}
, the optimization problem (
97
) can be rewritten as

\inf_{U\in\mathbb{R}^{I}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{U^{\top}A(D)U}{U^{\top}BU},\qquad A(D)=\sum_{n=1}^{N}D_{n}A_{n},

where the matrices
A_{1},\dots,A_{N}
and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{I\times I}
have entries

[A_{n}]_{i,j}=\int_{K_{n}}\varphi_{j}^{\prime}(q)\varphi_{i}^{\prime}(q)\mathrm{e}^{-V(q)}\,dq,\qquad B_{i,j}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\varphi_{j}(q)\varphi_{i}(q)\mathrm{e}^{-V(q)}\,dq.

(98)

The Euler–Lagrange condition for the minimization problem (
97
) is therefore the following finite dimensional eigenvalue problem:

A(D)U=\sigma BU,\qquad U^{\top}BU=1.

(99)

Since
A(D)
and
B
are real symmetric matrices and
B
is positive definite, the eigenvalue problem (
99
) admits
0\leqslant\sigma_{1}(D)\leqslant\sigma_{2}(D)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant\sigma_{I}(D)
real and nonnegative eigenvalues (counted without multiplicities), with associated
B
-orthonormal eigenvectors
\{U_{1}(D),U_{2}(D),\ldots,U_{I}(D)\}
. In addition,
\sigma_{1}(D)=0
and
U_{1}(D)
is proportional to
(1,\dots,1)
.

C.3
The discrete optimization problems

The optimization problem (
15
) is finally approximated by replacing
\Lambda(\mathscr{D}_{D})
in (
95
) by
\lambda_{N,I}(D)
. More precisely, fix
p\in[1,+\infty)
. For any
a,b\geqslant 0
such that (
94
) holds,

\text{Find }D^{\star}\in\delta^{p}_{a,b}\text{ such that }\sigma_{2}(D^{\star})=\sup_{D\in\delta_{p}^{a,b}}\sigma_{2}(D)=\lambda_{N,I}^{\star},

(100)

where
\sigma_{2}(D)
is the first nonzero (second-smallest) eigenvalue of the problem (
99
).

The following proposition guarantees the existence of a maximizer to (
100
), which is moreover positive under some compatibility on the meshes used to discretize the diffusion coefficient and the eigenvalue problem.

Proposition 25
.

Fix
p\in[1,+\infty)
. Consider
a,b\geqslant 0
such that (
94
) holds. Then, there exists
D^{\star}\in\delta_{p}^{a,b}
such that

\sigma_{2}(D^{\star})=\sup_{D\in\delta_{p}^{a,b}}\sigma_{2}(D)=\lambda_{N,I}^{\star}>0.

Assume moreover that, for all
n\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, there exists
i\in\{0,\dots,I-1\}
such that
[q_{i-1},q_{i+1}]\subset\overline{K_{n}}
. Then, any maximizer satisfies
\displaystyle\min_{1\leqslant n\leqslant N}D^{\star}_{n}>0
.

Proof.

As already mentioned in the proof of
Proposition

23
, the set
\delta_{p}^{a,b}
is compact. Thus, to prove the existence of a solution, it suffices to show that the application
D\mapsto\sigma_{2}(D)
is upper-semicontinuous; in fact, we prove that it is continuous. Indeed, the application
M\in\mathcal{S}_{I}(\mathbb{R})\mapsto\sigma_{2}(M)
mapping any symmetric matrix to its second-smallest eigenvalue is continuous on
\mathcal{S}_{I}(\mathbb{R})
(see
e.g.

[
12
, Corollary III.2.6]
). On the other hand,
D\mapsto A(D)
is also continuous. By composition,
D\mapsto\sigma_{2}(D)
is continuous on
\delta_{p}^{a,b}
, which proves the existence of a maximizer.

In addition, the Poincaré inequality (
61
) guarantees with the choice
D=\gamma\mathbbm{1}_{N}
(where
\mathbbm{1}_{N}
is the
N
-dimensional vector whose components are all equal to 1, and
\gamma>0
is a normalization constant chosen such that
D\in\delta_{p}^{a,b}
) that

\lambda_{N,I}^{\star}\geqslant\sigma_{2}(\gamma\mathbbm{1}_{N})\geqslant\frac{\gamma}{C_{\mu}}>0.

(101)

To prove that
D^{\star}_{n}>0
for all
n\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, we proceed by contradiction. Assume that there exists
n\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
such that
D^{\star}_{n}\leqslant 0
. Consider
i\in\{0,\ldots,I-1\}
such that
[q_{i-1},q_{i+1}]\subset\overline{K_{n}}
. Setting
\phi_{i}=\varphi_{i}-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\varphi_{i}\,\mu
(so that
\phi_{i}\in S_{I,0}
), we obtain

\sigma_{2}(D^{\star})\leqslant\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}D^{\star}(q)|\nabla\phi_{i}(q)|^{2}\mu(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\phi_{i}(q)^{2}\mu(q)\,dq}=D^{\star}_{n}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{K_{n}}|\nabla\phi_{i}(q)|^{2}\mu(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\phi_{i}(q)^{2}\mu(q)\,dq}\leqslant 0,

which contradicts (
101
) and thus proves the result.
∎

The optimization problem (
28
) is approximated as follows. Fix
p\in[1,+\infty)
. For any
a,b\geqslant 0
such that (
94
) holds,

\text{Find }D^{\star,\alpha}\in\delta^{p}_{a,b}\text{ such that }F_{\alpha}(D^{\star,\alpha})=\sup_{D\in\delta_{p}^{a,b}}F_{\alpha}(D)=\lambda_{N,I}^{\star,\alpha},

(102)

where

F_{\alpha}(D)=\frac{H_{\alpha}(D)}{G_{\alpha}(D)},

(103)

with

G_{\alpha}(D)=\sum_{i=2}^{I}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\sigma_{i}(D)},\qquad H_{\alpha}(D)=\sum_{i=2}^{I}\sigma_{i}(D)\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\sigma_{i}(D)}.

(104)

The well-posedness of the optimization problem (
102
) (without the positivity of the optimizer) can be obtained by similar arguments. Using similar (and even simpler) arguments as the ones presented after (
64
), one can show that

\forall\alpha>0,\qquad\lambda_{N,I}^{\star,\alpha}\geqslant\lambda_{N,I}^{\star},

and the map
\alpha\mapsto F_{\alpha}(D)
is nonincreasing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
. However, the monotonicity of the map
\alpha\mapsto\sigma_{2}(D^{\star,\alpha})
is not clear: it is observed numerically that this map is nondecreasing, see
Figures

2(b)
and
3(d)
.
Finally, let
(\alpha_{n})_{n\geqslant 0}
be a sequence of
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
such that
\alpha_{n}\to+\infty
when
n\to+\infty
. The sequence of maximizers
(D^{\star,\alpha_{n}})_{n\geqslant 0}
is bounded on
\delta_{p}^{a,b}
so that, upon extraction of a subsequence (not indicated in the notation), there exists
D^{\star,\infty}\in\delta_{p}^{a,b}
such that

D^{\star,\alpha_{n}}\xrightarrow[n\to+\infty]{}D^{\star,\infty}.

By definition, it holds

F_{\alpha_{n}}(D^{\star,\alpha_{n}})=\lambda_{N,I}^{\star,\alpha_{n}}\geqslant\lambda_{N,I}^{\star}\geqslant\sigma_{2}(D^{\star,\infty}).

By continuity of the maps
D\mapsto\sigma_{i}(D)
, it is then easy to show that
F_{\alpha_{n}}(D^{\star,\alpha_{n}})\to\sigma_{2}(D^{\star,\infty})
, so that
\lambda_{N,I}^{\star}=\sigma_{2}(D^{\star,\infty})
and
D^{\star,\infty}
solves (
100
). Moreover,
\lambda_{N,I}^{\star,\alpha}\to\lambda_{N,I}^{\star}
as
\alpha\to+\infty
. The latter convergence is illustrated in
Section

4
.

In conclusion, solving (
102
) for large values of
\alpha
should yield a tight upper bound on
\lambda_{N,I}^{\star}
. In practice, solving (
102
) for small values of
\alpha
already yields a good approximation in the situations we have considered.

C.4
Practical aspects of the implementation

We discuss in this section how to numerically solve (
15
), approximated as (
100
). The numerical solution of the smooth-min approximation (
28
), approximated as (
102
), is made precise in
Section

C.4.1
.

To solve (
100
) in practice, we used either (i) the Sequential Least Squares Quadratic Programming algorithm (SLSQP), which operates through linearization of the optimality conditions (see for instance
[
15
, Section 15.1]
for an introduction to such methods), relying on the implementation available in SciPy
[
78
]
; or (ii) the IPOPT solver
[
79
]
within the Julia programming language, using the JuMP framework
[
28
]
. In order to use these methods, we need to compute the gradient of the objective function and the constraint, which we discuss next.

A first task is to approximate the integrals for the matrix elements in (
98
) and the weight factors
\omega_{p,n}
in (
93
) by some quadrature method. In our numerical results, we set
N=I
and
K_{n}=[(n-1)/N,n/N)
for any
n\in\left\{1,\dots,N\right\}
, so that, for example,
[A_{n}]_{i,j}
can be approximated by (with periodic boundary conditions for the indices)

N\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{n-1}{N}\right)}(\delta_{i,n}\delta_{i,j}+\delta_{i,n-1}\delta_{i,j}-\delta_{i,n}\delta_{i,j+1}-\delta_{i,n-1}\delta_{i,j-1}),

(105)

where
\delta
is the Kronecker delta function, and
B_{i,j}
can be approximated by

\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{i-1}{N}\right)}\frac{4\delta_{i,j}+\delta_{i,j-1}+\delta_{i,j+1}}{6N}.

(106)

We refer to
Section

C.4.2
for more details in order to obtain (
105
)-(
106
). Likewise, the weights
\omega_{p,n}
can be approximated by

\frac{1}{N}\mathrm{e}^{-pV\left(\frac{n-1}{N}\right)}.

(107)

To run the optimization algorithms, we next need the gradient of the constraint

D\mapsto\sum_{n=1}^{N}\omega_{p,n}D_{n}^{p},

which is readily computed. A more subtle point is to compute the gradient of the target function
\lambda_{N,I}(D)=\sigma_{2}(D)
with the notation introduced in
Section

C.2
. It is readily computed assuming that the second eigenvalue
\sigma_{2}(D)
is simple as the following classical result shows. Recall that the matrix
B
is symmetric positive definite, so that the matrix
B^{-1/2}
is well-defined.

Proposition 26
.

Consider
D^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
such that the second-smallest eigenvalue
\sigma_{2}(D^{0})
of the symmetric positive matrix
B^{-1/2}A(D^{0})B^{-1/2}
is simple. Then,
D\mapsto\sigma_{2}(D)
is differentiable around
D^{0}
, and the components of its gradient are

\forall n\in\{1,\dots,N\},\qquad\frac{\partial\sigma_{2}}{\partial D_{n}}(D^{0})=U(D^{0})^{\top}A_{n}U(D^{0}),

(108)

where
U(D^{0})
is an eigenvector normalized as in (
99
).

Proof.

The proof involves manipulations similar to the ones used to prove
Proposition

5
. When
\sigma_{2}(D^{0})
is simple,
\sigma_{2}
is smooth in an open neighborhood of
D^{0}
, and
D\mapsto U(D)
can also be constructed to be smooth, so that, by taking the partial derivative with respect to
D_{n}
of the first condition in (
99
),

\frac{\partial A}{\partial D_{n}}(D)U(D)+A(D)\frac{\partial U}{\partial D_{n}}(D)=\frac{\partial\sigma_{2}}{\partial D_{n}}(D)BU(D)+\sigma_{2}(D)B\frac{\partial U}{\partial D_{n}}(D).

The desired result follows by multiplying this equality by
U(D)^{\top}
on the left, using the normalization
U(D)^{\top}BU(D)=1
and that
U(D)^{\top}A(D)=\sigma_{2}(D)U(D)^{\top}B
by transposing the relation (
99
) as the matrices
A(D)
and
B
are real symmetric.
∎

Using the approximation considered in (
105
) (recalling that
I=N
in this case), the formula approximating the gradient (
108
) reads: for any
n\in\left\{1,\dots,N\right\}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\sigma_{2}}{\partial D_{n}}(D)

\displaystyle\approx N\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{n-1}{N}\right)}\begin{pmatrix}U(D)_{n-1},U(D)_{n}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\
-1&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}U(D)_{n-1},U(D)_{n}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=N\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{n-1}{N}\right)}\left[U(D)_{n}-U(D)_{n-1}\right]^{2}.

(109)

Remark 9
.

Proposition

26
provides a formula for the gradient of
\sigma_{2}
around points where this function is differentiable, namely for
D\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
such that
\sigma_{2}(D)
is simple. However, formula (
108
) makes sense even if
\sigma_{2}(D)
is degenerate, although the resulting quantity is not necessarily the gradient of
\sigma_{2}
in this situation. In practice, we use this formula for the gradient for any
D
, including if degeneracy happens. The downside is that the convergence may be slower around degeneracy points.

Hyperparameters used to obtain the numerical results of
Section

4
.

We use a mesh characterized by
N=I=1000
(recall that
N
is the dimension of the approximation space for the diffusion coefficient
\mathcal{D}
, and that
I
is the number of basis functions for the
\mathbb{P}_{1}
finite element method). In particular, we choose
K_{n}=[(n-1)/N,n/N)
for
n\in\left\{1,\dots,N\right\}
. Note that we used
N=I
to simplify the implementation of
A_{n}
(see (
105
)) and the computation of the gradient of
\sigma_{2}
(see
Proposition

26
), even though the assumption in
Proposition

25
to ensure positivity does not hold in that case. Similar results were obtained using
I=kN
with
k\geqslant 2
which is a sufficient condition for the positivity result to hold. The integrals for the matrix elements in (
98
) are approximated by (
105
)-(
106
), and the weights
\omega_{p,n}
are approximated by (
107
). The initial condition for the diffusion coefficient in the optimization procedure solving either (
100
) or (
102
) is set to
D_{\mathrm{hom}}^{\star}
.

The constant
\gamma
used to defined the constant diffusion coefficient in
Section

4
is given by

\gamma=\left(\sum_{n=1}^{N}\omega_{p,n}\right)^{-1/p},

so that
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\omega_{p,n}D_{\mathrm{cst},n}^{p}=1
. Similarly, the constant
\widetilde{\gamma}_{\infty}
in (
35
) is determined by the equality
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\omega_{p,n}(D^{\star,\infty}_{n})^{p}=1
.

Remark 10
.

We have checked that all the numerical results conducted in
Sections

4
and
5.4
are converged up to numerical precision (with a convergence threshold of
10^{-15}
). For some potentials, we were not able to get converged results, in the sense that the convergence threshold was not attained before the maximum number of iterations, set to 1000 in our examples. In some cases, the algorithm even stopped before the maximum number of iterations, because it was unable to find a descent direction compatible with the constraints.
In practice, numerical convergence was harder to obtain when the optimal diffusion coefficient was almost zero at some points.

C.4.1
Numerical optimization of the smooth-min approximation

In order to solve (
102
), we need to compute the gradient of
F_{\alpha}
defined in (
103
) with respect to
D
. The expressions of the gradients of the eigenvalues with respect to
D
are similar to the expressions obtained in the proof of
Proposition

26
: if the maps
D\mapsto\sigma_{i}(D)
with
i\in\left\{2,\dots,I\right\}
are differentiable, one finds that

\forall n\in\left\{1,\dots,N\right\},\qquad\frac{\partial\sigma_{i}}{\partial D_{n}}(D)=U_{i}(D)^{\top}A_{n}U_{i}(D),

where
U_{i}(D)
is an eigenvector associated with
\sigma_{i}(D)
which satisfies
U_{i}(D)^{\top}BU_{i}(D)=1
. Likewise, using the approximation (
105
), and similarly to (
109
), it holds

\forall n\left\{1,\dots,N\right\},\qquad\frac{\partial\sigma_{i}}{\partial D_{n}}(D)\approx N\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{n-1}{N}\right)}[U_{i}(D)_{n}-U_{i}(D)_{n-1}]^{2}.

Then, it holds

\frac{\partial G_{\alpha}}{\partial D_{n}}(D)=-\alpha\sum_{i=2}^{I}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\sigma_{i}(D)}U_{i}(D)^{\top}A_{n}U_{i}(D),

and

\frac{\partial H_{\alpha}}{\partial D_{n}}(D)=\sum_{i=2}^{I}(1-\alpha\sigma_{i}(D))\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha\sigma_{i}(D)}U_{i}(D)^{\top}A_{n}U_{i}(D),

from which the gradient of
F_{\alpha}
is easily computed.

C.4.2
Approximation of the integral elements

Approximation of
A_{n}
.

Fix
n\in\left\{1,\dots,N\right\}
. On
K_{n}
, the only basis functions which are nonzero are
\varphi_{n}
and
\varphi_{n-1}
. Therefore, the only nonzero entries for
A_{n}
correspond to the (row, column) pairs
(n-1,n-1),(n-1,n),(n,n-1)
and
(n,n)
. For example, the entry
(n,n)
is approximated as

[A_{n}]_{n,n}=\int\limits_{\frac{n-1}{N}}^{n/N}(\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(q))^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-V(q)}\,dq\approx\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{n-1}{N}\right)}\int\limits_{\frac{n-1}{N}}^{\frac{n}{N}}(\varphi^{\prime}(q))^{2}\,dq=N\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{n-1}{N}\right)},

while the entry
(n,n-1)
is approximated as
[A_{n}]_{n,n-1}\approx-N\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{n-1}{N}\right)}
. The formula (
105
) then follows.

Approximation of
B
.

Fix
(i,j)\in\left\{1,\dots,N\right\}^{2}
. Similar approximations as above lead to

\displaystyle B_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{1}\varphi_{i}(q)\varphi_{j}(q)\mathrm{e}^{-V(q)}\,dq=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\int\limits_{\frac{n-1}{N}}^{\frac{n}{N}}\varphi_{i}(q)\varphi_{j}(q)\mathrm{e}^{-V(q)}\,dq

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{n-1}{N}\right)}\int\limits_{\frac{n-1}{N}}^{\frac{n}{N}}\varphi_{i}(q)\varphi_{j}(q)\,dq

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{n-1}{N}\right)}\left(2\delta_{j,n-1}\delta_{i,j}+\delta_{j,n-1}\delta_{i,j+1}+2\delta_{j,n}\delta_{i,j}+\delta_{j,n}\delta_{i,j-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6N}\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{i-1}{N}\right)}\left(2\delta_{i,j}+\delta_{i,j+1}\right)+\frac{1}{6N}\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{i}{N}\right)}\left(2\delta_{i,j}+\delta_{i,j-1}\right)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{6N}\mathrm{e}^{-V\left(\frac{i-1}{N}\right)}\left(4\delta_{i,j}+\delta_{i,j+1}+\delta_{i,j-1}\right),

which is exactly (
106
).

Appendix D
Proofs of the homogenization results

We fix
a,b>0
in all this section. The proofs of the results in
Sections

D.1
to
D.2
are given for completeness, as they are obtained by straightforward modifications of standard results of the literature on homogenization problems with Dirichlet boundary conditions
[
2
,
14
]
.

We start by stating a result which will be repeatedly used in the following, and whose proof is based on the Lax–Milgram lemma (by an easy adaptation of
[
2
, Lemma 1.3.21]
).

Lemma 27
.

Fix
a,b>0
, and consider
f\in H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that
\left\langle f,\mathbf{1}\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=0
and
\mathcal{A}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
. Then, there exists a unique solution
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to the problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\div{\mathcal{A}\nabla u}&=f&\text{in }H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

Moreover, there exists
C>0
(which does not depend on
u
nor
f
) such that

\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leqslant C\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

(110)

D.1
Proof of
Theorem

6

The proof boils down to adapting the proof of
[
2
, Theorem 1.2.16]
, which states a similar compactness result for equations with Dirichlet boundary conditions, to our case with periodic boundary conditions. To do so, we start by considering a modified problem on an extended domain with Dirichlet boundary conditions, and apply
[
2
, Theorem 1.2.16]
to obtain the
H
-convergence for this modified problem. We then use a result on the independence of
H
-convergence on boundary conditions, stated e.g. in
[
2
, Proposition 1.2.19]
, to obtain the
H
-convergence in the case of periodic boundary conditions.

Fix
f\in H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that
\langle f,\mathbf{1}\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=0
. Consider the open set
\Omega=(-1,2)^{d}
and denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{k}
and
\widetilde{f}
the extensions to
\Omega
of
\mathcal{A}^{k}
and
f
obtained by periodicity. Consider
\widetilde{u}_{0}^{k}\in H^{1}(\Omega)
the solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\div\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{u}_{0}^{k}\right)=\widetilde{f}&\quad\text{ on }\Omega,\\
\widetilde{u}_{0}^{k}=0&\quad\text{ on }\partial\Omega.\end{aligned}\right.

In view of
[
2
, Theorem 1.2.16]
, and upon extraction of a subsequence (not explicitly indicated), there exists
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega,\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
such that

\left\{\begin{aligned} \widetilde{u}^{k}_{0}&\rightharpoonup\widetilde{u}_{0}&\text{ weakly in }H^{1}(\Omega),\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{u}^{k}_{0}&\rightharpoonup\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\widetilde{u}_{0}&\text{ weakly in }L^{2}(\Omega)^{d},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\widetilde{u}_{0}
is the solution of the homogenized problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\div\left(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\widetilde{u}_{0}\right)=\widetilde{f}&\quad\text{ on }\Omega,\\
\widetilde{u}_{0}=0&\quad\text{ on }\partial\Omega.\end{aligned}\right.

We now turn back to our original problem with periodic boundary conditions. Denote by
u^{k}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
the unique solution (thanks to
Lemma

27
) to

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\div\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla u^{k}\right)=f&\quad\text{ on }\mathbb{T}^{d},\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{k}(q)\,dq=0.&\end{aligned}\right.

(111)

Since
\mathcal{A}^{k}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
and
u^{k}
has average 0 with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, standard estimates based on the Poincaré–Wirtinger inequality show that the sequence
(u^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
is uniformly bounded in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, hence weakly converges in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to a function
u
, up to the extraction of a subsequence. This extracted subsequence also strongly converges in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. In fact, as made precise at the end of the proof, we will prove that the whole sequence converges. With some abuse of notation, we therefore still denote the extracted subsequence as
(u^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
.
Note that, in particular,
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(q)\,dq=0
.

Denoting by
\widetilde{u}^{k},\widetilde{u}
the extensions to
\Omega
of
u^{k},u
obtained by periodicity, we claim that

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{u}^{k}\rightharpoonup\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\widetilde{u}\quad\text{ weakly in }L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)^{d}.

(112)

In view of
[
2
, Proposition 1.2.19]
, it suffices to this end to check that
\widetilde{u}^{k}\rightharpoonup\widetilde{u}
weakly in
H^{1}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)
and
-\div\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{u}^{k}\right)=\widetilde{f}\in H^{-1}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)
. To prove the first statement, we combine the weak convergence of
(u^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
with an argument based on a partition of unity. Fix
\widetilde{\phi}\in H^{-1}(\Omega)
with compact support
K
in
\Omega
. Thanks to the compactness of the support and for
\varepsilon\in(0,1/3)
fixed, there exists a finite number
L
of points
(x_{\ell})_{1\leqslant\ell\leqslant L}
such that
K\subset\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathcal{B}(x_{\ell},\varepsilon)
, where
\mathcal{B}(x_{\ell},\varepsilon)
denotes the open ball of radius
\varepsilon
centered at
x_{\ell}
. Upon reducing
\varepsilon
, it can be assumed that
\overline{\mathcal{B}(x_{\ell},\varepsilon)}\subset\Omega
for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
. Note that, for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
, the mapping
i_{\ell}:\mathcal{B}(x_{\ell},\varepsilon)\to\mathbb{T}^{d}
defined as
i_{\ell}(x)=x\text{ mod. }\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is injective. In addition, there exists an associated partition of unity, namely a family of nonnegative smooth functions
(\chi_{\ell})_{1\leqslant\ell\leqslant L}
such that
\supp(\chi_{\ell})\subset\overline{\mathcal{B}(x_{\ell},\varepsilon)}
for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
, and
\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\chi_{\ell}=1
on
K
. We use the decomposition
\widetilde{\phi}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\widetilde{\phi}_{\ell}
, where
\widetilde{\phi}_{\ell}=\widetilde{\phi}\chi_{\ell}
has support on
\overline{\mathcal{B}(x_{\ell},\varepsilon)}
. In particular, for all
k\geqslant 1
,

\left\langle\widetilde{\phi},\widetilde{u}^{k}\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\Omega),H^{1}(\Omega)}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\left\langle\widetilde{\phi}_{\ell},\widetilde{u}^{k}\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\Omega),H^{1}(\Omega)}.

Define next
\phi_{\ell}\in H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
from
\widetilde{\phi}_{\ell}
as

\forall v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\qquad\left\langle\phi_{\ell},v\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=\left\langle\widetilde{\phi},\chi_{\ell}(v\circ i_{\ell})\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\Omega),H^{1}(\Omega)}.

In particular,
\langle\widetilde{\phi}_{\ell},\widetilde{u}^{k}\rangle_{H^{-1}(\Omega),H^{1}(\Omega)}=\langle\phi_{\ell},u^{k}\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
for all
k\geqslant 1
and
\langle\widetilde{\phi}_{\ell},\widetilde{u}\rangle_{H^{-1}(\Omega),H^{1}(\Omega)}=\langle\phi_{\ell},u\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
, so that

\displaystyle\left\langle\widetilde{\phi},\widetilde{u}^{k}\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\Omega),H^{1}(\Omega)}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\left\langle\phi_{\ell},u^{k}\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\left\langle\phi_{\ell},u\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\left\langle\widetilde{\phi}_{\ell},\widetilde{u}\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\Omega),H^{1}(\Omega)}=\left\langle\widetilde{\phi},\widetilde{u}\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\Omega),H^{1}(\Omega)},

which allows to conclude that
\widetilde{u}^{k}\rightharpoonup\widetilde{u}
weakly in
H^{1}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)
. A similar argument, with the same partition of unity, can be used to prove that
-\div\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{u}^{k}\right)=\widetilde{f}
in
H^{-1}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)
for all
k\geqslant 1
.

For any
\Phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
, define the function
\Phi^{\dagger}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
obtained by extending
\Phi
by 0 outside of the set
(0,1)^{d}
identified with
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. More precisely,
\Phi^{\dagger}(q)=\mathbf{1}_{(0,1)^{d}}(q)\Phi(i(q))
, where
i:(0,1)^{d}\to\mathbb{T}^{d}
is the canonical injection. Note that
\Phi^{\dagger}\in L^{2}(\Omega)^{d}
has compact support in
\Omega
, and, using (
112
),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Phi(q)^{\top}\mathcal{A}^{k}(q)\nabla{u}^{k}(q)\,dq

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\Phi^{\dagger}(q)^{\top}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{k}(q)\nabla\widetilde{u}^{k}(q)\,dq

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[k\rightarrow+\infty]{}

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\Phi^{\dagger}(q)^{\top}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}(q)\nabla\widetilde{u}(q)\,dq=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\Phi(q)^{\top}\overline{\mathcal{A}}(q)\nabla{u}(q)\,dq,

where
\overline{\mathcal{A}}=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\circ i^{-1}
. This shows that
\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla{u}^{k}
weakly converges to
\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla{u}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
. In view of (
111
),

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\div\left(\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla u\right)=f&\quad\text{ in }H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(q)\,dq=0.&\end{aligned}\right.

Thus,
u
is uniquely defined, and the whole sequences
(u^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
and
(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla u^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
weakly converge respectively to
u
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla{u}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
, which provides the desired convergence in the sense of
Definition

1
.

D.2
Proof of
Theorem

8

The proof of
Theorem

8
is an adaptation of the proof of
[
2
, Theorem 1.3.16]
. The first step is to show that
(u^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
and
(\lambda^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
converge respectively (up to extraction) to an eigenvector and eigenvalue of the problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\div\left(\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla u(q)\right)&=\overline{\lambda}\rho(q)u(q)\quad\text{ on }\mathbb{T}^{d},\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u(q)^{2}\,dq&=1.\end{aligned}\right.

(113)

The second step is to prove that
\overline{\lambda}
is indeed the smallest nonzero eigenvalue of (
113
).

Convergence to a solution of the problem (
113
).

Let us first prove that, up to the extraction of a subsequence, the sequences
(\lambda^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
and
(u^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
respectively converge in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and weakly in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. By the min-max principle, using the fact that the eigenfunctions of the operator
-\div(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\cdot)
associated with the eigenvalue 0 are constant functions,
the second-smallest eigenvalue
\lambda^{k}
satisfies

\lambda^{k}=\min_{v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\setminus\{0\}}\left\{\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla v(q)^{\top}\mathcal{A}^{k}(q)\nabla v(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}v^{2}(q)\rho^{k}(q)\,dq}\ \middle|\ \int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}v\rho^{k}=0\right\}.

By ,
a\mathrm{Id}_{d}\leqslant\mathcal{A}^{k}\leqslant b^{-1}\mathrm{Id}_{d}
and
0<\rho_{-}\leqslant\rho^{k}\leqslant\rho_{+}
, so that, for all
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,

\frac{a}{\rho_{+}}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla v(q)|^{2}\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}v^{2}(q)\,dq}\leqslant\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla v(q)^{\top}\mathcal{A}^{k}(q)\nabla v(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho^{k}(q)v^{2}(q)\,dq}\leqslant\frac{1}{b\rho_{-}}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla v(q)|^{2}\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}v^{2}(q)\,dq},

which implies

0<\frac{4\pi^{2}a}{\rho_{+}}\leqslant\lambda^{k}\leqslant\frac{4\pi^{2}}{b\rho_{-}}

(114)

since the first nonzero eigenvalue of the operator
-\Delta
on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
is
4\pi^{2}
. This also implies

a\left\|\nabla u^{k}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}^{2}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u^{k}(q)^{\top}\mathcal{A}^{k}(q)\nabla u^{k}(q)\,dq=\lambda^{k}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho^{k}(q)u^{k}(q)^{2}\,dq\leqslant\frac{4\pi^{2}\rho_{+}}{b\rho_{-}},

since
u^{k}
is normalized in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. The sequences
(\lambda^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
and
(u^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
are therefore bounded respectively in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Upon extraction (not indicated explicitly in the notation), there exists a subsequence such that
\lambda^{k}\to\overline{\lambda}
, and
u^{k}\rightharpoonup u
weakly in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and strongly in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. In particular,
\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=1
. Since
\lambda^{k}\rho^{k}u^{k}
is the product of two converging subsequences, respectively for the weak-
*

L^{\infty}
topology and for the strong
L^{2}
topology, it converges to
\overline{\lambda}\rho u
weakly in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, and thus strongly in
H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Note also that

\lambda^{k}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho^{k}u^{k}=\left\langle-\div\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla u^{k}\right),\mathbf{1}\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=0,

so that, since
\lambda^{k}\neq 0
by (
114
) and
\rho^{k}u^{k}
weakly converges to
\rho u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
,

0=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho^{k}u^{k}=\left\langle\rho^{k}u^{k},\mathbf{1}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho u.

Since
\rho u
has integral 0, the solution
v^{k}
to the following problem is uniquely defined by
Lemma

27
:

-\div\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla v^{k}\right)=\overline{\lambda}\rho u,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}v^{k}=0.

By definition of the
H
-convergence
\mathcal{A}^{k}\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{H}\overline{\mathcal{A}}
, the sequence
(v^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
converges weakly in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to
v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, and
\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla v^{k}\rightharpoonup\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla v
weakly in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
, with
v
the unique solution in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to

-\div\left(\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla v\right)=\overline{\lambda}\rho u,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}v=0.

(115)

As

-\div\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\left(u^{k}-v^{k}-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{k}\right)\right)=\lambda^{k}\rho^{k}u^{k}-\overline{\lambda}\rho u\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}0

strongly in
H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, it follows by (
110
) that
u^{k}-v^{k}-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{k}\to 0
strongly in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Therefore, since
(u^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
weakly converges to
u
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, we obtain that
v^{k}\to u-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u
weakly in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. By uniqueness of the weak limit in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, it holds
v=u-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u
. By plugging this equality in (
115
), we can finally conclude that
(u^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
converges weakly in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to a solution
u
of (
113
).

Proving that
\overline{\lambda}
is the smallest nonzero eigenvalue.

To conclude the proof, we show that
\overline{\lambda}
is the smallest nonzero eigenvalue of (
113
), proceeding by contradiction. Assume that there exist
0<\widehat{\lambda}<\overline{\lambda}
and
\widehat{u}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\div\left(\overline{\mathcal{A}}(q)\nabla\widehat{u}(q)\right)&=\widehat{\lambda}\rho(q)\widehat{u}(q)\quad\text{ on }\mathbb{T}^{d},\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widehat{u}(q)^{2}\,dq&=1.\end{aligned}\right.

In particular,

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho\widehat{u}=0.

(116)

For
k\geqslant 1
, define
w^{k}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
as the unique solution to (well-defined by
Lemma

27
)

-\div(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla w^{k})=\widehat{\lambda}\rho\widehat{u},\qquad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}w^{k}=0.

(117)

The sequence
(w^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
is bounded in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
by (
110
), and hence weakly converges in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and strongly converges in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
up to extraction of a subsequence (not explicitly indicated). The limit
\widetilde{u}
satisfies, by definition of
H
-convergence,

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\div\left(\overline{\mathcal{A}}(q)\nabla\widetilde{u}(q)\right)&=\widehat{\lambda}\rho(q)\widehat{u}(q)\quad\text{ on }\mathbb{T}^{d},\\
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widetilde{u}(q)\,dq&=0.\end{aligned}\right.

(118)

The function
\widehat{u}-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widehat{u}
also satisfies (
118
), and is therefore equal to
\widetilde{u}
by uniqueness of the solution to (
118
). The second condition in (
118
) then implies that

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widehat{u}=-\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho\widetilde{u}}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho}.

(119)

Let us next introduce
\widetilde{w}^{k}=w^{k}-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho^{k}w^{k}/\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho^{k}
. Note that
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\rho^{k}\widetilde{w}^{k}=0
by construction and
\widetilde{w}^{k}\to\widetilde{u}+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widehat{u}=\widehat{u}
strongly in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
as
k\to+\infty
in view of (
119
). Then, using first the definition of
\lambda^{k}
, and next (
117
),

\displaystyle\lambda^{k}\leqslant\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}(q)^{\top}\mathcal{A}^{k}(q)\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widetilde{w}^{k}(q)^{2}\rho^{k}(q)\,dq}=\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla w^{k}(q)^{\top}\mathcal{A}^{k}(q)\nabla w^{k}(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widetilde{w}^{k}(q)^{2}\rho^{k}(q)\,dq}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\lambda}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}w^{k}(q)\widehat{u}(q)\rho(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widetilde{w}^{k}(q)^{2}\rho^{k}(q)\,dq}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}\widehat{\lambda}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widetilde{u}(q)\widehat{u}(q)\rho(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widehat{u}(q)^{2}\rho(q)\,dq}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\lambda}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widehat{u}(q)^{2}\rho(q)\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\widehat{u}(q)^{2}\rho(q)\,dq}=\widehat{\lambda},

where we used (
116
) in the last but one equality. We therefore obtain that
\overline{\lambda}\leqslant\widehat{\lambda}
, which contradicts the .

The argument above can in fact be applied to any converging subsequence of the original sequence
(\lambda^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
, henceforth leading to the uniqueness of the limit and the convergence of the whole sequence
(\lambda^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
, which completes the proof.

D.3
Proof of
Proposition

12

Consider a maximizer
\mathcal{D}_{0}\in\mathfrak{D}_{\#,k,p}^{a,b}
of (
54
) (which indeed exists in view of
Theorem

4
), and
u_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\setminus\{0\}
a minimizer associated with
\Lambda^{k}(\mathcal{D}_{0})
in (
53
). For
\ell=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{d})\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}^{d}
, introduce

\mathcal{D}_{\ell}(q)=\mathcal{D}_{0}\left(q+\frac{\ell}{k}\right),\qquad u_{\ell}(q)=u_{0}\left(q+\frac{\ell}{k}\right).

For these two functions we have, using the change of variable
Q=q+\ell/k
and the
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodicity of
V
,

\displaystyle\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u_{\ell}(q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}_{\ell}(q)\nabla u_{\ell}(q)\,\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\#,k}(q)}\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{\ell}(q)^{2}\,\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\#,k}(q)}\,dq}

\displaystyle=\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u_{0}\left(q+\frac{\ell}{k}\right)^{\top}\mathcal{D}_{0}\left(q+\frac{\ell}{k}\right)\nabla u_{0}\left(q+\frac{\ell}{k}\right)\,\mathrm{e}^{-V(kq)}\,dq}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{0}\left(q+\frac{\ell}{k}\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{e}^{-V(kq)}\,dq}

\displaystyle=\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u_{0}(Q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}_{0}(Q)\nabla u_{0}(Q)\,\mathrm{e}^{-V(kQ-\ell)}\,dQ}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u_{0}(Q)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-V(kQ-\ell)}\,dQ}=\Lambda^{k}(\mathcal{D}_{0}).

Thus, for all
\ell\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}^{d}
, the function
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
also satisfies
\Lambda^{k}(\mathcal{D}_{\ell})=\Lambda^{k,\star}
. Define now the diffusion matrix

\mathcal{D}_{0}^{k}(q)=\frac{1}{k^{d}}\sum_{\ell_{1}=0}^{k-1}\dots\sum_{\ell_{d}=0}^{k-1}\mathcal{D}_{\ell}(q).

By concavity of the application
\mathcal{D}\mapsto\Lambda^{k}(\mathcal{D})
(see
Lemma

3
),

\Lambda^{k}(\mathcal{D}_{0}^{k})\geqslant\frac{1}{k^{d}}\sum_{\ell_{1}=0}^{k-1}\dots\sum_{\ell_{d}=0}^{k-1}\Lambda^{k}(\mathcal{D}_{\ell})=\Lambda^{k,\star}.

Since
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{k}\in\mathfrak{D}_{\#,k,p}^{a,b}
by the triangle inequality,
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{k}
also satisfies
\Lambda^{k}(\mathcal{D}_{0}^{k})=\Lambda^{k,\star}
. In addition,
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{k}
is
(\mathbb{Z}/k)^{d}
-periodic by construction. There exists therefore
\mathcal{D}^{k,\star}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
such that
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{k}(q)=\mathcal{D}^{k,\star}(kq)
, which concludes the proof.

D.4
Proof of
Theorem

13

The following lemma, proved in
Section

D.4.1
, shows that in the particular case where one considers a sequence of diffusion matrices
(\mathcal{A}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
such that
\mathcal{A}^{k}
is
(\mathbb{Z}/k)^{d}
-periodic for any
k\geqslant 1
, the
H
-limit
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
is in fact a constant matrix. It also includes comparison results between weak-* and homogenized limits, which are needed in the proof of
Theorem

13
. This result is an adaptation to the case of periodic boundary conditions of standard results for divergence operators with Dirichlet boundary conditions (see for instance
[
2
, Lemmas 1.3.13 and 1.3.14]
).

Lemma 28
.

Let
a,b>0
and
(\mathcal{A}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
be a sequence of diffusion matrices such that
\mathcal{A}^{k}
is
(\mathbb{Z}/k)^{d}
-periodic for any
k\geqslant 1
. Then,
(\mathcal{A}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}

H
-converges (up to the extraction of a subsequence) to a constant matrix
\overline{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathcal{M}_{a,b}
.

Moreover, if
(\mathcal{B}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
is another sequence of diffusion matrices such that
\mathcal{B}^{k}
is
(\mathbb{Z}/k)^{d}
-periodic for any
k\geqslant 1
, and

\forall k\geqslant 1,\quad\forall\xi\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\qquad\xi^{\top}\mathcal{A}^{k}\xi\leqslant\xi^{\top}\mathcal{B}^{k}\xi,

then the homogenized limits are also ordered, that is, denoting by
\overline{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathcal{M}_{a,b}
the
H
-limit of
(\mathcal{B}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
,

\forall\xi\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\qquad\xi^{\top}\overline{\mathcal{A}}\xi\leqslant\xi^{\top}\overline{\mathcal{B}}\xi.

Furthermore, if

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\mathcal{A}_{+}\text{ weakly-* in }L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b}),

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\mathcal{A}_{-}^{-1}\text{ weakly-* in }L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b}),

then it holds

\forall\xi\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\qquad\xi^{\top}\mathcal{A}_{-}\xi\leqslant\xi^{\top}\overline{\mathcal{A}}\xi\leqslant\xi^{\top}\mathcal{A}_{+}\xi.

For fixed
k\geqslant 1
, recalling the notation
\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}(q)=\mathcal{D}(kq)
for any
\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
, we obtain by definition of the diffusion matrix
\mathcal{D}^{k,\star}
in
Proposition

12
that

\forall\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b},\qquad\Lambda^{k}(\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}^{k,\star})\geqslant\Lambda^{k}(\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}).

(120)

Fix now
\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
, and introduce the sequences
(\mathcal{A}^{k,\star})_{k\geqslant 1}
and
(\mathcal{A}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
defined as

\mathcal{A}^{k,\star}=\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}^{k,\star}\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\#,k}},\qquad\mathcal{A}^{k}=\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\#,k}}.

By
Lemma

28
, upon extraction a subsequence (still indexed by
k
with some abuse of notation), the sequence
(\mathcal{A}^{k,\star})_{k\geqslant 1}

H
-converges to a constant homogenized matrix
\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{\star}\in\mathcal{M}_{a,b}
. By
Theorem

7
, the sequence
(\mathcal{A}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
also
H
-converges to a constant matrix
\overline{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathcal{M}_{a,b}
. In addition, in view of the definitions (
43
) and (
53
),

\Lambda^{k}(\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}^{k,\star})=\Lambda_{\#,k}(\mathcal{D}^{k,\star}),\qquad\Lambda^{k}(\mathcal{D}_{\#,k})=\Lambda_{\#,k}(\mathcal{D}).

Now, by
Theorem

8
and
Corollary

9
,
H
-convergence implies the convergence of the associated spectral gap, and thus

\Lambda_{\#,k}(\mathcal{D}^{k,\star})\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}\overline{\Lambda}^{\star}=\min_{u\in H^{1,0}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla u^{\top}\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{\star}\nabla u}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{e}^{-V}},\qquad\qquad\Lambda_{\#,k}(\mathcal{D})\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{D}).

Thus, passing to the limit
k\to+\infty
in (
120
), we obtain that
\overline{\Lambda}^{\star}\geqslant\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{D})
. Since this result holds for any
\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
, we finally obtain that

\overline{\Lambda}^{\star}\geqslant\max_{\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{D})=\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}^{\star},

where we recall that
\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}^{\star}
is defined in (
47
).

To obtain the equality
\overline{\Lambda}^{\star}=\max_{\mathcal{D}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}}\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}(\mathcal{D})
, it therefore suffices to find a diffusion matrix
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
such that
\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}})=\overline{\Lambda}^{\star}
. The natural candidate is
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(q)=\mathrm{e}^{V(q)}\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{\star}
. Note first that
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\#,k}=\widehat{\mathcal{D}}_{\#,k}\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\#,k}}=\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{\star}
for any
k\geqslant 1
, so that the sequence
(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\#,k})_{k\geqslant 1}

H
-converges to
\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{\star}
, and therefore
\Lambda_{\mathrm{hom}}(\widehat{\mathcal{D}})=\overline{\Lambda}^{\star}
by
Corollary

9
. To conclude the proof, it suffices to check that
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
. Note first that, since
\mathcal{D}^{k,\star}\in\mathfrak{D}_{p}^{a,b}
, it holds for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and almost all
q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
,

a|\xi|^{2}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-V_{\#,k}(q)}\xi^{\top}\mathcal{D}_{\#,k}^{k,\star}(q)\xi\leqslant\frac{1}{b}|\xi|^{2}.

By
Lemma

28
, the above inequality passes to the
H
-limit, so that,

\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\qquad a|\xi|^{2}\leqslant\xi^{\top}\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{\star}\xi\leqslant\frac{1}{b}|\xi|^{2}.

Plugging
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(q)=\mathrm{e}^{V(q)}\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{\star}
in the last inequality allows to conclude that
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\in L^{\infty}_{V}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
. Let us next check that
