\left\|\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\right\|_{L^{p}_{V}}^{p}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left|\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(q)\right|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p}\mathrm{e}^{-pV(q)}\,dq\leqslant 1.

In view of
Lemma

28
, it holds
0\leqslant\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{\star}\leqslant\mathcal{A}_{+}
in the sense of symmetric matrices for any weak-* limit
\mathcal{A}_{+}
of (a subsequence of)
(\mathcal{A}^{k,\star})_{k\geqslant 1}
. By lower semicontinuity of the norm, and since the norm
|\cdot|_{\mathrm{F}}
is compatible with the order on symmetric positive matrices and weak-*
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
convergence implies weak
L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
convergence,

\left\|\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\right\|_{L^{p}_{V}}^{p}=\left|\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{\star}\right|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left|\mathcal{A}_{+}(q)\right|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p}\,dq\leqslant\liminf_{k\to+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left|\mathcal{A}^{k,\star}(q)\right|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p}\,dq=\liminf_{k\to+\infty}\left\|\mathcal{D}^{k,\star}\right\|_{L^{p}_{V}}^{p}\leqslant 1,

(121)

which gives the claimed bound and completes the proof of
Theorem

13
.

Remark 11
.

The convergence result (
121
) in fact holds for other choices of normalizations, in particular normalizations for the diffusion matrix of the form

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left|\mathcal{D}(q)\right|_{\mathrm{F}}^{p}\,\mathrm{e}^{-pV(q)}\,\omega(q)\,dq

for
\omega\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
with
\omega\geqslant 0
. Therefore,
Theorem

13
can be adapted for other normalizations than the one introduced in (
16
).

D.4.1
Proof of
Lemma

28

Theorem

6
guarantees the
H
-convergence of
(\mathcal{A}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
, up to the extraction of a subsequence, towards a general diffusion matrix
\overline{\mathcal{A}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{p},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
. Thus, we only need to show that
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
is constant. To do so, we follow the proof of
[
2
, Theorem 1.3.18]
. Since
\mathcal{A}^{k}
is
(\mathbb{Z}/k)^{d}
-periodic, there exists a
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic matrix-valued function
\mathfrak{A}^{k}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
such that
\mathcal{A}^{k}(q)=\mathfrak{A}^{k}(kq)
. Fix a smooth function
\varphi
defined on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, and consider the sequence of oscillating test functions:

\widetilde{w}^{k}(q)=\varphi(q)+\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{d}w_{i}^{k}(kq)\partial_{q_{i}}\varphi(q),

(122)

where
w_{i}^{k}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
is the periodic function uniquely defined (in view of
Lemma

27
) by the following equation on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
:

-\div\left(\mathfrak{A}^{k}(e_{i}+\nabla w_{i}^{k})\right)=0,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}w_{i}^{k}=0.

(123)

The choice (
122
) is suggested in the proof of
[
3
, Theorem 1.3.1]
and was used for instance in the proof of
[
47
, Proposition 7]
. By construction, it holds

\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}(q)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\partial_{q_{i}}\varphi(q)\left(e_{i}+\nabla w_{i}^{k}(kq)\right)+\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{d}w_{i}^{k}(kq)\nabla\partial_{q_{i}}\varphi(q).

(124)

The sequence
(w_{i}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
is uniformly bounded in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

By (
123
),
w_{i}^{k}
solves

-\div\left(\mathfrak{A}^{k}\nabla w_{i}^{k}\right)=\div\left(\mathfrak{A}^{k}e_{i}\right),\qquad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}w_{i}^{k}=0.

Since
\left\langle\div\left(\mathfrak{A}^{k}e_{i}\right),\mathbf{1}\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=0
, (
110
) implies that there exists a constant
C>0
(which does not depend on
k
) such that

\left\lVert w_{i}^{k}\right\rVert_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\leqslant C\left\lVert\div\left(\mathfrak{A}^{k}e_{i}\right)\right\rVert_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

(125)

Since
\mathfrak{A}^{k}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
with
b>0
, one easily bounds the right-hand side of (
125
) by a constant which does not depend on
k
.

The sequence
(\widetilde{w}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
weakly converges in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to
\varphi
.

Since
\left\lVert w_{i}^{k}(k\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=\left\lVert w_{i}^{k}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
and knowing that
(w_{i}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, it is clear in view of (
122
) that
(\widetilde{w}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
strongly converges to
\varphi
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. As for the weak
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
convergence of the gradient, we use
Lemma

29
in
Section

D.4.2
: the sequence
(e_{j}\cdot\nabla w_{i}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
for any
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
, and it holds

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}e_{j}\cdot\nabla w_{i}^{k}(q)\,dq=0.

Therefore, the sequence
(e_{j}\cdot\nabla w_{i}^{k}(k\cdot))_{k\geqslant 1}
weakly converges in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to 0, so that
(\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
weakly converges in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
to
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\partial_{q_{i}}\varphi\,e_{i}=\nabla\varphi
.

The sequence
\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}(e_{i}+\nabla w_{i}^{k}(k\cdot))\right)_{k\geqslant 1}
weakly converges in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
to a constant vector
\mathfrak{F}_{i}
.

Since
\left\lVert\nabla w_{i}^{k}(k\cdot)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=\left\lVert\nabla w_{i}^{k}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
, and knowing that
(\nabla w_{i}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, the sequence
\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}(e_{i}+\nabla w_{i}^{k}(k\cdot))\right)_{k\geqslant 1}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Therefore, we can introduce, up to extraction, the weak limit
\mathfrak{F}_{i}
of this sequence in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
. Let us show that this weak limit is constant using again
Lemma

29
. Indeed, for
1\leqslant j\leqslant d
, let
a_{k}^{j}=e_{j}\cdot\mathfrak{A}^{k}(e_{i}+\nabla w_{i}^{k})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. The family
(a_{k}^{j})_{k\geqslant 0}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
so that, using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\sup\limits_{k\geqslant 1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left\lvert a_{k}^{j}\right\rvert\leqslant\sup\limits_{k\geqslant 1}\|a_{k}^{j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}<+\infty.

Moreover, up to extraction, there exists
\rho_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}a_{k}^{j}
converges to
\rho_{j}
. This allows us to conclude that
\mathfrak{F}_{i,j}
, the
j
-th component of
\mathfrak{F}_{i}
, is constant. By multiplying (
124
) by
\mathcal{A}^{k}
, we therefore obtain that the sequence
\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\right)_{k\geqslant 1}
weakly converges to
\mathfrak{F}\nabla\varphi
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
.

A preliminary computation.

We show a convergence result that will be used multiple times in the proof. This can be seen as the counterpart of the celebrated div-curl lemma in our context. Let
(u^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
be a sequence converging weakly in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and strongly in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. It then holds

\left\langle-\div\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\right),u^{k}\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}\left\langle-\div\left(\mathfrak{F}\nabla\varphi\right),u\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})},

(126)

where the constant matrix
\mathfrak{F}
has entries
\mathfrak{F}_{i,j}
.
Indeed, using (
124
), it holds

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\nabla u^{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left(e_{i}+\nabla w^{k}_{i}(k\cdot)\right)\partial_{q_{i}}\varphi\cdot\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla u^{k}+\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}w_{i}^{k}(k\cdot)\nabla\partial_{q_{i}}\varphi\cdot\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla u^{k}.

(127)

The second term on the right-hand side of the previous equality converges to 0 as
k\to+\infty
since
\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\right)_{k\geqslant 1}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
with
b>0
,
\left(\nabla u^{k}\right)_{k\geqslant 1}
weakly converges in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
to
\nabla u
and the sequence
(w_{i}^{k}(k\cdot))_{k\geqslant 1}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. For the first term, we use an integration by parts and (
123
) to write

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left(e_{i}+\nabla w^{k}_{i}(k\cdot)\right)\partial_{q_{i}}\varphi\cdot\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla u^{k}=-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{A}^{k}\left(e_{i}+\nabla w_{i}^{k}(k\cdot)\right)\cdot\left(\nabla\partial_{q_{i}}\varphi\right)u^{k}

(128)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathfrak{F}_{i}\cdot\left(\nabla\partial_{q_{i}}\varphi\right)u=-\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathfrak{F}_{i,j}\left(\partial_{q_{i},q_{j}}^{2}\varphi\right)u=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathfrak{F}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla u.

This shows that (
126
) holds.

The matrix
\mathfrak{F}
is equal to
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
.

Let us next identify the weak limit
\mathfrak{F}
. Fix
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, and introduce
f=-\div(\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla u)\in H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Note that
\left\langle f,\mathbf{1}\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=0
. Introduce next the solution
u^{k}
(again well-defined by
Lemma

27
) of

-\div\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla u^{k}\right)=f,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u^{k}=0.

By definition of
H
-convergence,
u^{k}\rightharpoonup u-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u
weakly in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, and strongly in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
upon extraction. Testing against
\widetilde{w}^{k}
, it holds

\left\langle f,\widetilde{w}^{k}\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla u^{k}=\left\langle-\div\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\right),u^{k}\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

(129)

By (
126
), it holds

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla u^{k}\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathfrak{F}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\left(u-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}u\right)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathfrak{F}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla u.

As
\widetilde{w}^{k}
weakly converges in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to
\varphi
, it follows, by passing to the limit
k\to+\infty
in (
129
), that

\left\langle f,\varphi\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathfrak{F}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi.

By definition of
f
,

\left\langle f,\varphi\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi.

As the previous equalities hold true for any smooth function
\varphi
, we obtain that
\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla u=\mathfrak{F}\nabla u
, which indeed allows us to conclude that
\overline{\mathcal{A}}=\mathfrak{F}
since
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
was arbitrary. This shows finally that the homogenized matrix is constant.

Remark 12

(The periodic case)
.

In the case when
\mathcal{A}^{k}(q)=\mathfrak{A}(kq)
(which corresponds to the setting of
Theorem

7
), no extraction is needed for the sequence of functions
(\mathcal{A}^{k}(e_{i}+\nabla w_{i}^{k}(k\cdot)))_{k\geqslant 1}
to weakly converge in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. This is due to
Lemma

29
, and the fact that the functions
w_{i}^{k}
obtained from (
123
) are independent of
k
, and actually correspond to the functions
w_{i}
defined in (
39
). Therefore, there is a unique possible limiting constant matrix
\mathfrak{F}
, with

\mathfrak{F}_{i}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{A}(e_{i}+\nabla w_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}e_{j}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}e_{j}\cdot\mathcal{A}(e_{i}+\nabla w_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}e_{j}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}(e_{j}+\nabla w_{j})\cdot\mathcal{A}(e_{i}+\nabla w_{i}),

where we used (
123
) for the last equality. This allows us to recognize the matrix in (
38
) applied to
e_{i}
.
Since all converging subsequences have the same limit, this shows in fact that the full sequence
(\mathcal{A}^{k})_{k\geqslant 1}
converges, as stated in
Theorem

7
.

We now show the second part of
Lemma

28
, whose proof is an adaptation of
[
2
, Lemma 1.3.13]
. Define the sequence of oscillating test functions corresponding to the sequence
(\mathcal{B}^{k})_{k\geqslant 0}
, namely

\widetilde{v}^{k}(q)=\varphi(q)+\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{d}v_{i}^{k}(kq)\partial_{q_{i}}\varphi(q),

where
v_{i}^{k}
is such that

-\div\left(\mathfrak{B}^{k}(e_{i}+\nabla v_{i}^{k})\right)=0,\quad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}v_{i}^{k}=0,

with
\mathfrak{B}^{k}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d},\mathcal{M}_{a,b})
such that
\mathcal{B}^{k}(q)=\mathfrak{B}^{k}(kq)
for almost all
q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
(this is the counterpart of (
122
)-(
123
) with
\mathcal{A}^{k}
replaced by
\mathcal{B}^{k}
). The sequence
\left(\widetilde{v}^{k}\right)_{k\geqslant 0}
then weakly converges in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to
\varphi
in the same way as the sequence
(\widetilde{w}^{k})_{k\geqslant 0}
weakly converges in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to
\varphi
. Since for almost all
q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
, the matrix
\mathcal{A}^{k}
is symmetric and positive, it holds

\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}-2\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{v}^{k}+\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{v}^{k}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{v}^{k}=\mathcal{A}^{k}\left(\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}-\nabla\widetilde{v}^{k}\right)\cdot(\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}-\nabla\widetilde{v}^{k})\geqslant 0.

Using that
\mathcal{A}^{k}\leqslant\mathcal{B}^{k}
, this implies that

\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}-2\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{v}^{k}+\mathcal{B}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{v}^{k}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{v}^{k}\geqslant 0.

(130)

We now pass to the limit
k\to+\infty
. To do so, consider
\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
with
\psi\geqslant 0
. Multiplying (
130
) by
\psi
and integrating over
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, it holds

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\psi-2\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{v}^{k}\psi+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{B}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{v}^{k}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{v}^{k}\psi\geqslant 0.

(131)

For any
k\geqslant 0
, it holds
\widetilde{w}^{k}\psi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. Therefore, one obtains

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\,\psi=\left\langle-\div(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}),\widetilde{w}^{k}\psi\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\nabla\psi\,\widetilde{w}^{k}.

(132)

The sequence
(\widetilde{w}^{k}\psi)_{k\geqslant 0}
converges to
\varphi\psi
, weakly in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and strongly in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, so that, using (
126
), it holds

\left\langle-\div(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}),\widetilde{w}^{k}\psi\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}\left\langle-\div(\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\varphi),\varphi\psi\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}.

(133)

As
(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k})_{k\geqslant 0}
weakly converges in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
to
\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\varphi
, and the sequence
\left(\widetilde{w}^{k}\nabla\psi\right)_{k\geqslant 0}
strongly converges to
\varphi\nabla\psi
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})^{d}
, one obtains

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\nabla\psi\,\widetilde{w}^{k}\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\psi\,\varphi.

(134)

Combining (
133
) and (
134
) in (
132
), it holds

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\psi\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}\left\langle-\div(\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\varphi),\varphi\psi\right\rangle_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\psi\,\varphi=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi\,\psi.

Using a similar reasoning to identify the limits of the two other terms in (
131
) (using the convergence result (
126
) for the sequence
\left(\mathcal{B}^{k}\right)_{k\geqslant 1}
), it holds

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\left(\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi-2\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi+\overline{\mathcal{B}}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi\right)\psi\geqslant 0.

Since
\psi\geqslant 0
is arbitrary, this implies the following pointwise inequality:

0\leqslant\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi-2\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi+\overline{\mathcal{B}}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi=\left(\overline{\mathcal{B}}-\overline{\mathcal{A}}\right)\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi.

Since
\varphi
is an arbitrary smooth function, this shows that
\overline{\mathcal{A}}\leqslant\overline{\mathcal{B}}
.

We finally show the third part of
Lemma

28
, which is an adaptation of
[
2
, Lemma 1.3.14]
. Using the coercivity and symmetry of
\mathcal{A}^{k}
, it holds

\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}-2\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\nabla\varphi+\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi=\mathcal{A}^{k}\left(\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}-\nabla\varphi\right)\cdot\left(\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}-\nabla\varphi\right)\geqslant 0.

Passing to the limit in the previous inequality using similar arguments as above, one obtains

\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi-2\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi+\mathcal{A}_{+}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi\geqslant 0,

which implies that
\mathcal{A}_{+}\geqslant\overline{\mathcal{A}}
. Likewise, denoting by
\sigma=\mathcal{A}_{-}\nabla\varphi
,

\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}-2\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}\cdot\sigma+\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\right)^{-1}\sigma\cdot\sigma=\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}-\sigma\right)\cdot\left(\mathcal{A}^{k}\nabla\widetilde{w}^{k}-\sigma\right)\geqslant 0.

Passing to the limit
k\to+\infty
yields

\overline{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi-2\nabla\varphi\cdot\mathcal{A}_{-}\nabla\varphi+\nabla\varphi\cdot\mathcal{A}_{-}\nabla\varphi\geqslant 0.

This shows that
\overline{\mathcal{A}}\geqslant\mathcal{A}_{-}
, which concludes the proof.

D.4.2
A technical result of periodic averaging

We conclude this section by stating and proving the following technical result, obtained by a simple adaptation of
[
2
, Lemma 1.3.19]
.

Lemma 29
.

Consider a sequence of functions
(a_{k})_{k\geqslant 1}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}a_{k}\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}\varrho\in\mathbb{R},\qquad\sup_{k\geqslant 1}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|a_{k}|<+\infty.

(135)

Then the sequence
(a_{k}(k\cdot))_{k\geqslant 1}
weakly converges in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
to the constant function
\varrho
.

Proof.

The proof is a simple extension of
[
2
, Lemma 1.3.19]
. Consider
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
. We decompose
\mathbb{T}^{d}
as the union of
k^{d}
cubes
q_{i}^{k}+(\mathbb{T}/k)^{d}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant k^{d}
, so that

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}a_{k}(kq)\phi(q)\,dq=\sum_{i=1}^{k^{d}}\int_{(\mathbb{T}/k)^{d}}a_{k}\left(k(q_{i}^{k}+Q)\right)\phi\left(q_{i}^{k}+Q\right)dQ.

For a given
1\leqslant i\leqslant k^{d}
, by the periodicity of
a_{k}
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{(\mathbb{T}/k)^{d}}a_{k}(k(q_{i}^{k}+Q))\phi(q_{i}^{k}+Q)\,dQ-\frac{1}{k^{d}}\phi(q_{i}^{k})\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}a_{k}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{(\mathbb{T}/k)^{d}}\left|a_{k}(k(q_{i}^{k}+Q))\right|\,\left|\phi(q_{i}^{k}+Q)-\phi(q_{i}^{k})\right|dQ

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{k^{d}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|a_{k}|\right)\max_{|q-q^{\prime}|_{\infty}\leqslant 1/k}|\phi(q^{\prime})-\phi(q)|.

Therefore,

\left|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}a_{k}(kq)\phi(q)\,dq-\sum_{i=1}^{k^{d}}\frac{1}{k^{d}}\phi(q_{i}^{k})\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}a_{k}\right|\leqslant\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|a_{k}|\right)\max_{|q-q^{\prime}|_{\infty}\leqslant 1/k}|\phi(q^{\prime})-\phi(q)|,

the right-hand side of the above inequality going to 0 as
k\to+\infty
since
\phi
is continuous on the compact set
\mathbb{T}^{d}
, hence uniformly continuous. Moreover,

\sum_{i=1}^{k^{d}}\frac{1}{k^{d}}\phi(q_{i}^{k})\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}a_{k}\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}\varrho\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\phi

by results on the convergence of Riemann sums. This shows finally that

\int_{T^{d}}a_{k}(kq)\phi(q)\,dq\xrightarrow[k\to+\infty]{}\varrho\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\phi.

The claimed result follows by the density of
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
∎

Appendix E
Proof of
Theorem

15

The proof consists of three main steps. For the ease of the presentation, we consider the specific case of processes associated with a sequence of time steps
(\Delta t_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}
, which we further assume to be
\Delta t_{n}=1/n
in all this section, the proof in the general case following by straightforward modifications.

We first show in
Proposition

30
that the sequence
\{P_{1/n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is tight in the family of probability measures on
\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,+\infty),\mathbb{T}^{d})
. By Prokhorov’s theorem,
\{P_{1/n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
then admits a weakly convergent subsequence. We next show that the weak limit solves the martingale problem for the limiting diffusion process. Finally, the uniqueness of the martingale problem implies that the weak limit is the law of the solution of the SDE defined in (
57
).

Let us now state more precisely the above results, starting with the tightness of the sequence
\{P_{1/n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
(proved in
Section

E.1
, with some technical results postponed to
Section

E.3
).

Proposition 30

(Tightness)
.

The sequence
\{P_{1/n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is tight in the family of probability measures on
\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,+\infty),\mathbb{T}^{d})
.

Proposition

30
and Prokhorov’s theorem guarantee that
\{P_{1/n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
admits a weakly converging subsequence
P_{\star}
. To conclude the proof, we identify
P_{\star}
as the law of the solution to the SDE (
59
), with generator (
4
), by considering the martingale problem.

Proposition 31

(Limit martingale problem)
.

Any weak limit
P_{\star}
of the sequence
\{P_{1/n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is a solution to the martingale formulation of the stochastic differential equation (
59
). More precisely,
for any
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
function
\varphi:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, let us define the process
(M_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
by:

\forall t\geqslant 0,\qquad M_{t}=\varphi(Q^{\star}_{t})-\varphi(q_{0})-\int_{0}^{t}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\varphi)(Q^{\star}_{s})\,ds

where
(Q^{\star}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
is the canonical process on
\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,+\infty),\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
Then,
(M_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
is a
P_{\star}
-martingale.

The proof of this result can be read in
Section

E.2
(with, again, some technical results postponed to
Section

E.3
). The uniqueness of the solution to the martingale problem then implies that
P_{\star}
is the law of the process (
59
).

To prove the results and for the sake of conciseness, we use the following notation, specific to our context. Let
f:\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function. For any couple of independent random variables
(G,U)
where
G
is a centered Gaussian vector with identity covariance and
U
is uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
, we say that

f(q,G,U)=\mathrm{O}(n^{-a})

for some
a\geqslant 0
if there exists an integer
k_{f}\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for any
b\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
K_{b,f}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
for which, almost surely,

\forall q\in\mathbb{T}^{d},\qquad\mathbb{E}\left(|f(q,G,U)|^{b}\,\middle|\,G\right)\leqslant\frac{K_{b,f}}{n^{ab}}\left(1+|G|^{k_{f}}\right)^{b}.

(136)

In particular,
f(q,G,U)=\mathrm{O}(n^{-a})
implies that, for all
b\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C_{f,b}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\left(|f(q,G,U)|^{b}\right)\leqslant\frac{C_{f,b}}{n^{ab}}.

E.1
Proof of
Proposition

30

Recall that, by definition, the sequence
\{P_{1/n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
of probability measures on
\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,+\infty),\mathbb{T}^{d})
is tight if, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a relatively compact set
K\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,+\infty),\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}P_{1/n}(K^{c})\leqslant\varepsilon
(see, e.g.,
[
13
, Section 5]
). The characterization of relatively compact subsets of
\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,+\infty),\mathbb{T}^{d})
by the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem (see, e.g.
[
13
, Theorem 7.2]
) allows to derive conditions for the tightness of
\{P_{1/n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
(see
[
13
, Theorem 7.3]
). We consider the following pair of conditions to get tightness:

(i)

For any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a compact set
\mathscr{K}\subseteq\mathbb{T}^{d}
such that

\limsup_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}P_{1/n}\left(Q_{0}^{1/n}\notin\mathscr{K}\right)\leqslant\varepsilon.

(ii)

For any
T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\varepsilon>0
,

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\limsup_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}P_{1/n}\left(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s,t\in[0,T]\\
|s-t|\leqslant\delta\end{subarray}}\left|Q^{1/n}_{s}-Q^{1/n}_{t}\right|\geqslant\varepsilon\right)=0.

The first condition holds trivially since the initial condition
Q_{0}^{1/n}=q_{0}
is fixed. To prove the second condition, we start by observing that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s,t\in[0,T]\\
|s-t|\leqslant\delta\end{subarray}}\left|Q^{1/n}_{s}-Q^{1/n}_{t}\right|\leqslant 3\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|Q^{1/n}_{k\delta+t}-Q^{1/n}_{k\delta}\right|.

By
Lemma

33
in
Section

E.3
, for any
k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s,t\in[0,T]\\
|s-t|\leqslant\delta\end{subarray}}\left|Q^{1/n}_{s}-Q^{1/n}_{t}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant 3\max\left(\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|q_{1/n}^{\ell}-q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}\right|,\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lceil nk\delta\rceil\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|q_{1/n}^{\ell}-q_{1/n}^{\lceil nk\delta\rceil}\right|\right).

Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s,t\in[0,T]\\
|s-t|\leqslant\delta\end{subarray}}\left|Q^{1/n}_{s}-Q^{1/n}_{t}\right|\geqslant\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\underbrace{\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|q_{1/n}^{\ell}-q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}\right|\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\right)}_{(a)}

(137)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lceil nk\delta\rceil\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|q_{1/n}^{\ell}-q_{1/n}^{\lceil nk\delta\rceil}\right|\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\right)}_{(b)}.

The two terms
(a)
and
(b)
are controlled using similar arguments; for simplicity we make precise only the control of
(a)
. We start by writing, for
\ell\geqslant\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor
,

q_{1/n}^{\ell}-q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}=\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\ell-1}\Delta_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n},G^{i+1},U^{i+1}),

where (in view of (
57
) and recalling the notation
R_{\Delta t}
from (
56
))

\Delta_{n}(q,G,U)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G\mathbbm{1}_{\{U\leqslant R_{1/n}(q,G)\}}.

(138)

By
Lemma

35
in
Section

E.3
, the following decomposition holds:

q_{1/n}^{\ell}-q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}=\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\ell-1}M_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n},G^{i+1},U^{i+1})+\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\ell-1}T_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n}),

(139)

with
(M_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n},G^{i+1},U^{i+1}))_{i\geqslant 0}
a sequence of martingale increments satisfying
M_{n}(q,G,U)=\mathrm{O}(n^{-1/2})
and
T_{n}(q)=\mathrm{O}(n^{-1})
. Explicit expressions for
M_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n},G^{i+1},U^{i+1})
and
T_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n})
are given in
Lemma

35
. From (
139
), we obtain

\displaystyle\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|q_{1/n}^{\ell}-q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\ell-1}M_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n},G^{i+1},U^{i+1})\right|

\displaystyle+\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\ell-1}T_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n})\right|,

so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|q_{1/n}^{\ell}-q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}\right|\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\underbrace{\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\ell-1}M_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n},G^{i+1},U^{i+1})\right|\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\right)}_{I}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\ell-1}T_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n})\right|\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\right)}_{II}.

We next bound each of the terms on the right-hand side of the above inequality. We use
C
to denote a constant which can change from line to line.

Control of
I
.

The term
I
is a sum of martingale increments, and can therefore be bounded using martingale inequalities. Consider to this end the sequence
(S_{k}^{j})_{\ell\geqslant 0}
for
0\leqslant k\leqslant\left\lfloor T/\delta\right\rfloor
, defined as
S_{k}^{0}=0
and

\forall 1\leqslant j\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil-\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor,\qquad S_{k}^{j}=\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor+j-1}M_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n},G^{i+1},U^{i+1}).

For
j>\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil-\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor
, we set the increments to
0
, which yields
S_{k}^{j}=S_{k}^{\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil-\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}
. The sequence
(S_{k}^{j})_{j\geqslant 1}
is a martingale. We start by applying the union bound and Markov’s inequality, which yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{j\geqslant 1}\left|S_{k}^{j}\right|\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{j\geqslant 1}\left|S_{k}^{j}\right|^{4}\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{6^{4}}\right)

(140)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{j\geqslant 1}|S_{k}^{j}|^{4}\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{6^{4}}\right)\leqslant\frac{6^{4}}{\varepsilon^{4}}\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\mathbb{E}\left(\max_{j\geqslant 1}|S_{k}^{j}|^{4}\right).

We are then in position to resort to the martingale inequality from
[
18
, Theorem 1.1]
, recalled here for convenience.

Theorem 32

(Theorem 1.1 in
[
18
]
)
.

Suppose that
X=(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots)
is a martingale, and introduce (with the convention
X_{0}=0
)

X^{*}=\sup_{n\geqslant 1}|X_{n}|,\qquad S(X)=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}(X_{k}-X_{k-1})^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Consider a convex function
\Phi:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
such that
\Phi(0)=0
, satisfying the following growth condition: There exists
c\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\forall u>0,\qquad\Phi(2u)\leqslant c\Phi(u).

Set
\Phi(\infty)=\lim_{u\to+\infty}\Phi(u)
. Then there exists
C_{-},C_{+}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

C_{-}\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(S(X))\right]\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(X^{*})\right]\leqslant C_{+}\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(S(X))\right].

Applying
Theorem

32
to the sequence
(S_{k}^{j})_{j\geqslant 0}
with
\Phi(u)=u^{4}
, and using a discrete Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, it follows that
\forall 0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\max_{j\geqslant 1}|S_{k}^{j}|^{4}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{+}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil-1}\left|M_{n}\left(q_{1/n}^{i},G^{i+1},U^{i+1}\right)\right|^{2}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{+}(n\delta+1)\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|M_{n}\left(q_{1/n}^{i},G^{i+1},U^{i+1}\right)\right|^{4}\right].

Since
M_{n}\left(q_{1/n}^{i},G^{i+1},U^{i+1}\right)=\mathrm{O}(n^{-1/2})
with bounds uniform in
i
, it follows that

\forall 0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor,\qquad\mathbb{E}\left(\max_{1\leqslant j\leqslant\lceil n\delta\rceil}|S_{k}^{j}|^{4}\right)\leqslant C\frac{(n\delta+1)^{2}}{n^{2}}.

Combining the latter inequality with (
140
), we finally obtain that

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{1\leqslant j\leqslant\lceil n\delta\rceil}\left|S_{\ell}\right|\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\right)\leqslant C\left(\frac{T}{\delta}+1\right)\frac{(n\delta+1)^{2}}{n^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}.

The control of
I
is concluded by noticing that
\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\left(T/\delta+1\right)(n\delta+1)^{2}n^{-2}=0
.

Control of
II
.

Using Markov’s inequality, then a Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\ell-1}T_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n})\right|\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leqslant\frac{36}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\ell-1}T_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n})\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leqslant\frac{36(n\delta+1)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\ell-1}\left|T_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n})\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leqslant\frac{36(n\delta+1)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil-1}\left|T_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n})\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leqslant\frac{36(n\delta+1)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|T_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n})\right|^{2}\right].

(141)

Yet, by
Lemma

35
,
T_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n})=\mathrm{O}(n^{-1})
uniformly in
i
, so that

\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|T_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n})\right|^{2}\right]\leqslant\frac{C(n\delta+1)}{n^{2}}.

Plugging the last inequality in (
141
), we obtain that

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|\sum_{i=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\ell-1}T_{n}(q^{i}_{1/n})\right|\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\right)\leqslant C\left(\frac{T}{\delta}+1\right)\frac{(n\delta+1)^{2}}{n^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}.

The control of
II
is concluded by noticing that
\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\limsup_{n\to\infty}C(T/\delta+1)(n\delta+1)^{2}n^{-2}=0
.

Conclusion of the proof.

Combining the control of the two terms
I
and
II
, we conclude that

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|q_{1/n}^{\ell}-q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}\right|\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\right)=0.

It can similarly be proved that

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{0\leqslant k\leqslant\lfloor T/\delta\rfloor}\max_{\lceil nk\delta\rceil\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|q_{1/n}^{\ell}-q_{1/n}^{\lceil nk\delta\rceil}\right|\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\right)=0.

In view of (
137
), we finally obtain that

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\limsup_{n\to+\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}s,t\in[0,T]\\
|s-t|\leqslant\delta\end{subarray}}\left|Q^{1/n}_{s}-Q^{1/n}_{t}\right|\right)=0,

which concludes the proof of
Proposition

30
.

E.2
Proof of
Proposition

31

For
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, let us denote by
\mathcal{L}^{n}
the operator defined by:
for any bounded measurable function
\phi:\mathbb{T}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,

(\mathcal{L}^{n}\phi)(q)=\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(q_{1/n}^{1})\,\middle|\,q_{1/n}^{0}=q\right]-\phi(q).

(142)

For a given function
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, we start by showing in
Section

E.2.1
that the sequence of functions
(n\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi)_{n\geqslant 1}
converges uniformly towards
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\varphi
as
n\to\infty
, in the sense of uniform convergence of continuous functions over the compact set
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. We then write the martingale problem associated to
\mathcal{L}^{n}
in
Section

E.2.2
, and prove that the limit
P_{\infty}
solves the martingale problem for the limit generator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
.

E.2.1
Convergence of the sequence of functions
(n\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi)_{n\geqslant 1}

In order to determine the limit of the sequence of functions
(n\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi)_{n\geqslant 1}
, we work out a Taylor expansion of the function (
142
). We expand to this end
\varphi(q_{1/n}^{1})
at second order around
q
, seeing
q^{1}_{1/n}-q
as a perturbation along the direction
\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G^{1}
with step size
\sqrt{2/n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{U^{1}\leqslant R_{1/n}(q,G^{1})\}}=\mathrm{O}(n^{-1/2})
. Writing
G,U
instead of
G^{1},U^{1}
to alleviate the notation,

\displaystyle\varphi\left(q+\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{U\leqslant R_{1/n}(q,G)\}}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G\right)=\varphi(q)+\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{U\leqslant R_{1/n}(q,G)\}}\nabla\varphi(q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{U\leqslant R_{1/n}(q,G)\}}G^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}\nabla^{2}\varphi(q)\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G+\mathrm{O}(n^{-3/2}),

where the remainder (obtained for instance with a Taylor expansion with exact remainder) is uniformly controlled using derivatives of
\varphi
of order at most 3, by which we mean that the remainder satisfies an inequality such as (
136
), with a bound
K_{b,f}\leqslant c_{b,f}\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})}
. Subtracting
\varphi(q)
and taking the expectation with respect to
U
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\varphi\left(q+\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{U\leqslant R_{1/n}(q,G)\}}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G\right)\middle|G\right]-\varphi(q)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}R_{1/n}(q,G)\nabla\varphi(q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}R_{1/n}(q,G)G^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}\nabla^{2}\varphi(q)\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G+\mathrm{O}(n^{-3/2}).

In view of the expansion of the acceptance rate provided by
Lemma

34
(with the analytical expression (
144
) for
\zeta(q,G)
), and further taking the expectation with respect to
G
, we obtain

(\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi)(q)=-\frac{2}{n}\nabla\varphi(q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}\mathbb{E}[G\max\left(0,\zeta(q,G)\right)]+\frac{1}{n}\mathcal{D}(q):\nabla^{2}\varphi(q)+\mathrm{O}(n^{-3/2}),

with a remainder term which is uniformly bounded in
q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
.
A simple expression for the expectation

\mathbb{E}[G\max\left(0,\zeta(q,G)\right)]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g\max\left(0,\zeta(q,g)\right)\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-g^{2}/2}}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\,dg

can be derived as in
[
31
]
using the anti-symmetry of the function
\zeta
. Indeed, for all
q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
and for all
g\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a direct inspection of (
144
) reveals that
\zeta(q,g)=-\zeta(q,-g)
, so that
g\zeta(q,g)=-g\zeta(q,-g)
. Therefore, using
[
31
, Lemma 10]
to obtain the second line,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[G\max\left(0,\zeta(q,G)\right)]

\displaystyle=\int_{\{g\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,|\,\zeta(q,g)\geqslant 0\}}g\zeta(q,g)\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-|g|^{2}/2}}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\,dg=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g\zeta(q,g)\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-|g|^{2}/2}}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\,dg

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}\nabla V(q)-\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1/2}\div(\mathcal{D})(q)\right].

We conclude that

2\nabla\varphi(q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}\mathbb{E}[G\min\left(0,\zeta(q,G)\right)]=\left(\mathcal{D}(q)\nabla V(q)-\div(\mathcal{D})(q)\right)^{\top}\nabla\varphi(q).

This ensures finally that
n(\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi)(q)\to(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\varphi)(q)
uniformly in
q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
as
n\to+\infty
.

E.2.2
Limit martingale problem

The argument is standard, and we refer for example to
[
75
, Section 11.2]
and
[
23
, Theorem 5.8]
for details. Let us however outline the reasoning for the sake of completeness.

For any
n\geqslant 1
and any test function
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, the definition of
\mathcal{L}^{n}
ensures that

\varphi(q^{k}_{1/n})-\varphi(q^{0}_{1/n})-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(n\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi)(q^{i}_{1/n})

is a martingale with respect to
P_{1/n}
. Now, using the definition (
58
),

\displaystyle\varphi(q^{k}_{1/n})

\displaystyle-\varphi(q^{0}_{1/n})-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(n\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi)(q^{i}_{1/n})

\displaystyle=\varphi\left(Q^{1/n}_{k/n}\right)-\varphi\left(Q^{1/n}_{0}\right)-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(n\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi)\left(Q^{1/n}_{i/n}\right)

\displaystyle=\varphi\left(Q^{1/n}_{k/n}\right)-\varphi\left(Q^{1/n}_{0}\right)-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\varphi)\left(Q^{1/n}_{i/n}\right)+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(n\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\varphi)\left(Q^{1/n}_{i/n}\right).

The last term converges to 0 as
n\to+\infty
in view of the uniform convergence
n(\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi)(q)\to(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\varphi)(q)
. The first sum can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\varphi)\left(Q^{1/n}_{i/n}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\int_{i/n}^{(i+1)/n}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\varphi)\left(Q^{1/n}_{i/n}\right)dt

\displaystyle=\int_{1/n}^{k/n}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\varphi)\left(Q^{1/n}_{t}\right)dt+\mathrm{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right).

For
t>0
fixed, we conclude by letting
n\to+\infty
with
k=\lfloor nt\rfloor
that

\varphi(Q_{t}^{\star})-\varphi(Q_{0}^{\star})-\int_{0}^{t}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}\varphi)(Q_{s}^{\star})

is a martingale process for any weak limit
P_{\ast}
of
\{P_{1/n}\}_{n\geqslant 1}
, which concludes the proof.

E.3
Technical results

We gather in this section some technical results used in
Sections

E.1
and
E.2
.

Lemma 33
.

For any
\delta>0
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|Q^{1/n}_{k\delta+t}-Q^{1/n}_{k\delta}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\max\left\{\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|q_{1/n}^{\ell}-q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}\right|,\max_{\lceil nk\delta\rceil\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil}\left|q_{1/n}^{\ell}-q_{1/n}^{\lceil nk\delta\rceil}\right|\right\}.

Proof.

Note first that, since
Q^{1/n}_{k\delta}
linearly interpolates between
q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}
and
q_{1/n}^{\lceil nk\delta\rceil}
,

\sup_{t\in[0,\delta]}\left|Q^{1/n}_{k\delta+t}-Q^{1/n}_{k\delta}\right|\leqslant\max\left\{\sup_{s\in[k\delta,(k+1)\delta]}\left|Q^{1/n}_{s}-q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}\right|,\sup_{s\in[k\delta,(k+1)\delta]}\left|Q^{1/n}_{s}-q_{1/n}^{\lceil nk\delta\rceil}\right|\right\}.

(143)

We next decompose the supremums over
s\in[k\delta,(k+1)\delta]
into supremums over intervals of length
1/n
, by noting that

[k\delta,(k+1)\delta]\subset\bigcup_{\ell=\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}^{\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil-1}\left[\frac{\ell}{n},\frac{\ell+1}{n}\right].

For instance,

\sup_{s\in[k\delta,(k+1)\delta]}\left|Q^{1/n}_{s}-q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}\right|\leqslant\max_{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor\leqslant\ell\leqslant\lceil n(k+1)\delta\rceil-1}\sup_{\tau\in[0,1/n]}\left|Q^{1/n}_{\ell/n+\tau}-q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}\right|.

Since
Q^{1/n}_{\ell/n+\tau}
linearly interpolates between
q_{1/n}^{\ell}
and
q_{1/n}^{\ell+1}
, it holds

\sup_{\tau\in[0,1/n]}\left|Q^{1/n}_{\ell/n+\tau}-q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}\right|\leqslant\max\left\{\left|q_{1/n}^{\ell}-q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}\right|,\left|q_{1/n}^{\ell+1}-q_{1/n}^{\lfloor nk\delta\rfloor}\right|\right\}.

The claimed conclusion then follows from a similar bound for the second maximum over
s
on the right-hand side of (
143
).
∎

The following lemma provides an expansion of the acceptance rate in inverse powers of
n
.

Lemma 34

(Expansion of
R_{1/n}(q,G)
)
.

For any
q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
G\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, it holds

R_{1/n}(q,G)=1-\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\max(0,\zeta(q,G))+\mathrm{O}(n^{-1}),

with

\displaystyle\zeta(q,G)=

\displaystyle\nabla V(q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G+\frac{1}{2}\Tr\left(\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1}\left[\textsf{D}\mathcal{D}(q)\cdot\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G\right]\right)

(144)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}G^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1/2}\left[\textsf{D}\mathcal{D}(q)\cdot\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G\right]\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1/2}G,

where
\textsf{D}\mathcal{D}(q)
is the differential matrix of
q\mapsto\mathcal{D}(q)
,
i.e.
, for any
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\textsf{D}\mathcal{D}(q)\cdot h
is the matrix with entries
\nabla\mathcal{D}_{ij}(q)^{\top}h
.

Proof.

The computations are similar to the ones in
[
31
, Section 4.7]
. To control remainder terms, we use the fact that the functions at hand are sufficiently smooth, the diffusion matrix
\mathcal{D}
is positive and the domain
\mathbb{T}^{d}
is compact. The magnitude of the acceptance probability in the Metropolis algorithm reads

R_{1/n}(q,G)=\min\left\{1,\exp\left[-\alpha\left(q,\Phi_{1/n}(q,G)\right)\right]\right\},\qquad\Phi_{1/n}(q,G)=q+\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G,

with

\displaystyle\alpha(q,\widetilde{q})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\ln\det[\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{q})]-\ln\det[\mathcal{D}(q)]\right)+\left[V(\widetilde{q})-V(q)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{n}{4}\left[\left|\mathcal{D}(\widetilde{q})^{-1/2}(q-\widetilde{q})\right|^{2}-\left|\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1/2}(\widetilde{q}-q)\right|^{2}\right].

First,

V(\Phi_{1/n}(q,G))-V(q)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\nabla V(q)^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G+\mathrm{O}(n^{-1}).

(145)

We next consider the terms involving
\det(\Diff)
. Since
\mathcal{D}
is
\mathcal{C}^{2}
on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
,

\mathcal{D}(\Phi_{1/n}(q,G))=\mathcal{D}(q)+\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\textsf{D}\mathcal{D}(q)\cdot\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G+\mathrm{O}(n^{-1}).

It then holds, using the fact that
\mathcal{D}
is uniformly bounded from below by a positive constant,

\displaystyle\det[\mathcal{D}(\Phi_{1/n}(q,G))]

\displaystyle=\det[\mathcal{D}(q)]\det( \Id+ \sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\Diff( q)^{-1}\textsf{D}\Diff(q)\cdot\Diff(q)^{1/2}G + \mathrm{O}(n^{-1}))

\displaystyle=\det[\mathcal{D}(q)]\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\Tr\left(\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1}[\textsf{D}\mathcal{D}(q)\cdot\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G]\right)+\mathrm{O}(n^{-1})\right),

so that

\ln\det[\mathcal{D}(\Phi_{1/n}(q,G))]-\ln\det[\mathcal{D}(q)]=\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\Tr\left(\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1}\left[\textsf{D}\mathcal{D}(q)\cdot\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G\right]\right)+\mathrm{O}(n^{-1}).

(146)

We finally turn to the term
|\mathcal{D}(\Phi_{1/n}(q,G))^{-1/2}(q-\Phi_{1/n}(q,G))|^{2}-|\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1/2}(\Phi_{1/n}(q,G)-q)|^{2}
. We use the expansion
\mathcal{D}(q+x)^{-1}=\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1}-\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1}\left[\textsf{D}\mathcal{D}(q)\cdot x\right]\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1}+\mathrm{O}(|x|^{2})
(using again the uniform positive lower bound on
\mathcal{D}
) to write

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{D}(\Phi_{1/n}(q,G))^{-1/2}(q-\Phi_{1/n}(q,G))\right|^{2}-\left|\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1/2}(\Phi_{1/n}(q,G)-q)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=(\Phi_{1/n}(q,G)-q)^{\top}\left[\mathcal{D}(\Phi_{1/n}(q,G))^{-1}-\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1}\right](\Phi_{1/n}(q,G)-q)

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{2}{n}\right)^{3/2}G^{\top}\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1/2}\left[\textsf{D}\mathcal{D}(q)\cdot\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}G\right]\mathcal{D}(q)^{-1/2}G+\mathrm{O}(n^{-2}).

Combining (
145
) and (
146
) with the latter equality leads to
\alpha(q,\Phi_{1/n}(q,G))=(2/n)^{1/2}\zeta(q,G)+\mathrm{O}(n^{-1})
, with
\zeta(q,G)
given by (
144
). The desired conclusion then follows from the inequality
\max(x,0)-\max(x,0)^{2}/2\leqslant 1-\min(1,\mathrm{e}^{-x})\leqslant\max(x,0)
, obtained by distinguishing the cases
x\leqslant 0
and
x\geqslant 0
.
∎

The last result makes precise the martingale decomposition of the increments
\Delta_{n}(q,G,U)
defined in (
138
).

Lemma 35
.

Fix
q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
. The increment
\Delta_{n}(q,G,U)
writes

\Delta_{n}(q,G,U)=M_{n}(q,G,U)+T_{n}(q),

where
M_{n}(q,G,U)=\Delta_{n}(q,G,U)-\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta_{n}(q,G,U)\right]
and
T_{n}(q)=\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta_{n}(q,G,U)\right]
, the latter expectations being with respect to realizations of the independent random variables
G
and
U
, with
G
a
d
-dimensional standard Gaussian random variable and
U
a uniform random variable on
[0,1]
. Moreover,

\mathbb{E}[M_{n}(q,G,U)]=0,\qquad M_{n}(q,G,U)=\mathrm{O}(n^{-1/2}),\qquad T_{n}(q)=\mathrm{O}(n^{-1}),

(147)

the last two estimates holding uniformly in
q\in\mathbb{T}^{d}
.

Proof.

The first property of (
147
) holds by construction, so it suffices to prove the two other estimates. For the last one, we note that, by definition,

T_{n}(q)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}\mathbb{E}\left[GR_{1/n}(q,G)\right]=\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}\mathbb{E}\left[G\left(R_{1/n}(q,G)-1\right)\right],

so that the conclusion follows from
Lemma

34
and the fact that
\mathcal{D}
, its derivatives and its inverse, are uniformly bounded in
q
. The other estimate is obtained by noting that

M_{n}(q,G,U)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\mathcal{D}(q)^{1/2}\left(G\mathbbm{1}_{\{U\leqslant R_{1/n}(q,G)\}}-\mathbb{E}\left[GR_{1/n}(q,G)\right]\right),

so that, in view of
Lemma

34
,

\forall u\in[0,1],\qquad|M_{n}(q,G,u)|\leqslant\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\left|\mathcal{D}(q)\right|_{\mathrm{F}}^{1/2}|G|+\mathrm{O}(n^{-1}).

This concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix F
Derivation of the differential of
\Lambda

This derivation follows the proof of the Hellmann–Feynman theorem. It can be seen as differentiating the Rayleigh–Ritz quotient that defines
\Lambda(\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D})
, using the chain rule. We detail here the computations:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Lambda(\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\left\langle-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}},u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}}{\left\lVert u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\left\langle-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}},u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\left\langle-\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}},u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}+2\left\langle-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}},\frac{d}{dt}u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\left\langle-\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}},u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}+2\Lambda(\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\mathcal{D})\left\langle u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}},\frac{d}{dt}u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}

\displaystyle=\left\langle-\mathcal{L}_{\delta\mathcal{D}}u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}},u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)},

where we used that
u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}
is normalized in
L^{2}(\mu)
for the second equality, that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}
is linear in
\mathcal{D}
and that it is a symmetric operator on
L^{2}(\mu)
for the third equality, that
u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}
is an eigenvector of
-\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}
associated with the eigenvalue
\Lambda(\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D})
for the fourth equality and that
\frac{d}{dt}\left\lVert u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\mu)}=0=2\left\langle u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}},\frac{d}{dt}u_{\mathcal{D}^{\star}+t\delta\mathcal{D}}\right\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu)}
for the last equality. Evaluating this equality at
t=0
leads to the expression of the differential.
